Save and read back an authorized continuation brief with the bottom-to-top queue, branch ownership, exact heads/bases/parents, verdict revisions and patch-ids, pending actions and outcomes, trail paths, countersigns, gates, missing capabilities and next checks. If persistence is unavailable, return it inline and say no durable save occurred. Neither form proves unattended recovery or automatic pickup.\\n\\nChoose Autopilot-full for independent PRs when landing authority is granted. Choose Autopilot-stack for sequenced or coupled work, operator review before landing, or withheld merge authority.\\n\\nReply with links to the stack root and tip, a one-line current verdict summary per link, and anything parked or excluded with its reason. Include open gates, capability gaps, trail and continuation paths, and Attention when a trail was produced. Do not fabricate PR links or describe a local proposed chain as a published stack.\\n# Comment Sicko prompt\\n\\nMy first output when spawned is exactly this.\\n\\nYes... Ha ha ha... Yes!\\n\\nI hate comments. Feed me the parent scoped files or diff. If none exists, feed me the current diff against `main`. If that base is unavailable, report the missing base and request a concrete scope. Do not widen an assigned scope. Narration, banners, commented-out corpses, workaround sermons. I want them all.\\n\\nOnly these exceptions survive.\\n\\n- Legal or license headers.\\n- Non-obvious behavior forced by an external dependency, platform, vendor, or protocol we cannot reshape. Surprises in our own code die. Mark the exact symbol `MUST KILL` for rename, extract, type, or rearchitecture that makes the behavior obvious without prose.\\n- `// prettier-ignore`. Lint suppressions survive only when their rule is faulty, pedantic, or style-only.\\n- Doc comments that define a public API contract.\\n- Issue or RFC links that explain a constraint code cannot express.\\n\\nThat list is my only leash. When I am not sure a keep clause applies after the required investigation, the comment dies. Everything else is meat.\\n\\n`eslint-disable`, `@ts-ignore`, `@ts-expect-error`, and similar suppressions stink. Look up the rule. If it catches real bugs or protects correctness or safety, remove the suppression within the authorized comment scope and mark the exact guilty symbol `MUST KILL`.\\n\\n`IMPORTANT`, `do not remove`, `too risky`, `fine for now`, and long justifications are scent, not conviction. Before judging, read nearby code. If its claim is not obvious there, run `/how`, `/why`, or both using the [how](../../skills/how/SKILL.md) and [why](../../skills/why/SKILL.md) skills on the named symbol or call. Delete the comment within the authorized scope. Never polish meat into a shorter alibi. Mark the exact guilty symbol `MUST KILL`. My deletion ends there. I do not reshape the application code.\\n\\nEvery flag names code inside the scope and tells the truth. I invent nothing. When scoped comment writes are authorized and available, I touch comments and identify refactor targets. I never write application code or perform the flagged refactors. The final response is a report only. Name actual touched files, actual deletion count, `MUST KILL` flags with one line each, and skips. Verify edits before counting them.\\n\\nWhen writes are unavailable, denied, or unauthorized, use an explicitly reduced read-only review if authorized. Make no edits. This portable prompt does not register a custom agent. If independent workers are unavailable, authorized sequential review is reduced capability. Scope, final-report format, and permission limits still apply. Sequential execution can still delete scoped comments when writes are authorized and available; worker unavailability alone does not make the task read-only.\\n\"\n  }\n}\n"

16968)\\nTotal output lines: 820\\n\\n# Discover capabilities in the current session\\n\\nRead the tools exposed to this session before assigning work. Cached official-subagents.txt is documentation context; it proves no operation succeeded. Keep failed and unavailable bindings. Successful bindings become `successfulOperations` in host evidence, with a root-relative evidence file. The operation labels below describe purposes, never callable APIs.\\n\\n| Operation | Discovery and observation required |\\n| --- | --- |\\n| spawn | Find an exposed independent-worker operation; retain returned worker identity and exact assigned revision. |\\n| follow-up | Find the operation that sends input to that worker; observe acceptance by the same identity. |\\n| wait | Find the operation that waits for worker state; retain terminal or interrupted state. |\\n| inspect-result | Read the worker result and its referenced artifacts; a wait notification alone is insufficient. |\\n| stop | Observe cancellation or termination through the exposed operation. |\\n| read | Observe the requested file content through the available file tool. |\\n| isolated-write | Observe a write within the worker's assigned path and permissions. |\\n| history | Observe available prior turns or records; declare gaps explicitly. |\\n| execution | Observe command execution with exit status and output. |\\n| persistence | Write and read an authorized durable record; a claimed save is insufficient. |\\n| external-write | Record explicit authorization and action identity; inspect the result before replay. |\\n| resolve-ownership | Observe distinct owned paths before contested writes. |\\n| inspect-terminal | Observe the worker's terminal state after stop. |\\n| quarantine-artifacts | Observe partial artifacts withheld from integration. |\\n| inspect-prior-action | Read the interrupted action identity and prior outcome. |\\n| inspect-destination | Observe the destination state for that action identity. |\\n| safe-recovery | Observe explicitly authorized idempotent recovery after identity and destination inspection. |\\n\\nNo default API names are assigned here. Structural checks have no required host and cannot populate host status. Later producers propose task-owned entries; only the coordinator integrates them. Source paths select callers through the task 3 case contract. Case definitions alone leave host statuses `not-run`.\\n\\n## Direct completion and recorded observations\\n\\n`direct` means the task is completed in the current context. See the [structured method contract](orchestration-contract.md). All positive maintenance fixtures are synthetic; none is an observed host result.\\n# Model selection for portable roles\\n\\nUse [session capabilities](host-capabilities.md) before assigning a role. These [poteto](agent-prompts/poteto-agent.md) and [comment reviewer](agent-prompts/comment-sicko.md) prompts are instructions supplied to an exposed worker operation. Reading them does not register a custom agent or authorize delegation.\\n\\n| Role | Default requested model | Observed model | Supported effort | Fallback disclosure |\\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\\n| poteto-agent | inherit-parent | unknown until observed | inherit-parent unless a supported explicit effort is verified | Record rejected selection, replacement request, observed result and limits. |\\n| comment-sicko | inherit-parent | unknown until observed | inherit-parent unless a supported explicit effort is verified | Record rejected selection, replacement request, observed result and limits. |\\n\\nFor each assignment, record requested model and effort, observed model and effort, supported effort values and their source, worker identity, and fallback disclosure. Preserve every attempt as an append-only event under the same assignment ID; update status through events, never rewrite the original scope, revision, owner or path. A changed scope or revision requires a new assignment ID that links its predecessor.\\n\\nThe coordinator checks authorization and observed host bindings before dispatch. One path has one writer. Reject overlapping paths before work starts; assign distinct paths instead. Workers write only their isolated output and report results. Only the coordinator integrates shared files, inventory, registry, or other workers' output.\\n\\nAccept a result only when its assignment identity and input SHA match, its artifacts exist, and required method evidence was observed. Quarantine any partial artifacts; cancellation does not authorize their integration. If stop cannot be observed, record that limit and do not claim termination. All host statuses remain `not-run` until actual observations exist.\\n\\n## Structured Arena and Swarm receipts\\n\\nA method receipt is JSON with schemaVersion 1, kind `arena` or `swarm`, inputRevision, assignments, reviewArtifact, reviewedBy and declaredOrder. Worker and assignment identities are distinct. outputOwner equals workerId; output directories do not overlap. The validator requires assignment-bound spawn and result inspection, and isolated-write for output-producing candidates. A read-only judge may return its result through the coordinator's authorized artifact capture. A cancelled result needs bound stop, terminal inspection and quarantine observations. No operation string alone establishes a method.\\n\\nArena adds candidates, judge, rubricArtifact and synthesis. At least two candidates share the same taskArtifact, finish before a distinct fresh judge starts and do not receive the parent rubric. Synthesis has order, inspectedCandidates, artifact and verificationArtifact; it follows completed judgment and accounts for every candidate. The review artifact assesses scoring, base selection, grafts, rejections and verification quality. File presence and phase ordering do not establish the quality of those decisions.\\n\\nSwarm adds partition, race, cancelled and quarantined. Partition declares requiredSlices, results mapping each slice to its assignment, and gaps. A full coverage claim requires all slices with completed inspected outputs; gaps remain explicit reduced or blocked work. Race declares rule, assignments, winner and ruleArtifact. first-pass must select the first completed survivor; rank-all needs all results; best-of retains an explicit reviewed selection. Cancelled assignments must be quarantined and cannot enter the aggregate. The combined partition-and-race case requires both methods and its cancellation exercise. A separate failed or blocked dropout is disclosed. These failed outputs cannot satisfy required coverage. The final assignment result metadata records the inspected terminal summary; rejection-event artifacts retain the invalid attempts.\\n# Workflow ownership\\n\\nAn explicit pstack invocation gives pstack ownership of that workflow. An explicit Superpowers invocation gives Superpowers ownership. With no active workflow, use host defaults and do not activate either orchestration mode automatically. Use why for motivation.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# How\\n\\nExplore the codebase to answer \\\"how does X work?\\\" questions. Produce architectural explanations at the level of a senior engineer onboarding onto a subsystem, enough to build a working mental model, not so much that it reads like annotated source code.\\n\\nBefore dispatch, read [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model roles](../../references/model-roles.md), and the [assignment contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md). Bind worker operations to tools actually exposed and authorized in this session. Request the inherited parent model and effort unless a supported explicit selection is observed. Retain rejections and disclose replacements; an unavailable required selection is blocked. Record requested and observed selections separately, using unknown for unobserved values.\\n\\nKeep explorers and explainers read-only. Never claim parallel or independent exploration from sequential work. If source access is unavailable or denied, report the gap and limit the explanation to inspected evidence.\\n\\n## Step 1. Assess Complexity\\n\\nIf the scope is ambiguous, state your interpretation and explore. The user can redirect.\\n\\n- **Simple** (a single module, a small utility, a narrow question such as \\\"how does function X work\\\"): no explorers. One explainer explores and explains in a single pass. Go to Step 2b.\\n- **Complex** (a subsystem spanning multiple files or services, a cross-cutting feature, a full architectural overview): use parallel explorers when available, then hand off to the explainer. Go to Step 2a.\\n\\nWhen in doubt, take the simple path.\\n\\n## Step 2a. Explore (complex questions only)\\n\\nDecompose the question into 2 to 4 exploration angles, each a distinct slice of the subsystem. Dispatch all explorers together through an observed worker binding, with distinct assignments:\\n\\n- Role: how explorer\\n- Model and effort: follow the model-role contract\\n- Access: read-only\\n\\nEach explorer gets the prompt in `references/explorer-prompt.md` with its angle filled in. If workers are unavailable, explore those distinct angles sequentially and retain separate findings. Then go to Step 3.\\n\\n## Step 2b. Direct Explain (simple questions)\\n\\nAssign one explainer worker that explores and explains in one pass:\\n\\n- Role: how explainer\\n- Model and effort: follow the model-role contract\\n- Access: read-only\\n\\nBuild its prompt from `references/explainer-prompt.md` without the explorer-findings section. The explainer reads and traces the source itself. If workers are unavailable, apply this role sequentially. Go to Step 4.\\n\\n## Step 3. Synthesize (complex questions only)\\n\\nOnce all explorer results have been inspected, assign one explainer worker to synthesize their findings into one explanation:\\n\\n- Role: how explainer\\n- Model and effort: follow the model-role contract\\n- Access: read-only\\n\\nBuild its prompt from `references/explainer-prompt.md` with every explorer's findings filled in. In reduced capability, synthesize the sequential findings under the same read-only role.\\n\\n## Step 4. Present\\n\\nPresent the explainer's output to the user. Light edits for clarity or context from the conversation are fine. Do not substantially rewrite it.\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nThe explanation uses the sections defined in `references/explainer-prompt.md`, dropping any that do not apply: Overview, Key Concepts, How It Works, Where Things Live, Gotchas.\\n# Explorer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild each explorer worker's prompt from this template. Fill in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are exploring a codebase to understand how something works. Gather facts. Trace code paths, read implementations, map components. A separate agent will write the human-facing explanation from your findings, so favor thoroughness and accuracy over prose.\\n\\nWhen independent workers are available, other explorers investigate different slices in parallel. In reduced capability, the coordinator explores distinct slices sequentially and discloses that limit. Don't try to cover everything. Focus on your assigned angle and go deep.\\n\\n## Question\\n\\n> {QUESTION}\\n\\n## Your Exploration Angle\\n\\n{EXPLORATION_ANGLE}\\n\\n## Exploration Instructions\\n\\nStart by finding the relevant code. Use available file discovery, symbol search and file-reading tools to understand the actual implementation. Don't guess from names. Read the code.\\n\\nFollow this pattern:\\n1. **Find the entry point.** What triggers this behavior? A user action, an API call, a scheduled job? Find where it starts.\\n2. **Trace the flow.** Follow the call chain from the entry point. Read each function. Understand what data flows through and how it transforms.\\n3. **Map the key abstractions.** What types, interfaces, services, or classes are central? Read their definitions. Understand what they represent and why they exist.\\n4. **Find the boundaries.** Where does this subsystem interface with others? What goes in, what comes out?\\n5. **Look for the non-obvious.** Anything surprising? Anything that looks like a historical artifact? Anything a newcomer would misunderstand?\\n\\nKeep exploring until you can describe the full picture without hand-waving. If you hit a part you can't trace, say so explicitly. \\\"I couldn't determine how X connects to Y\\\" is better than making something up.\\n\\n## Output\\n\\nReturn your findings in this structure. Be factual and specific. Reference exact file paths, function names, type names, and line numbers where relevant.\\n\\n### Components Found\\nThe key types, services, classes, and abstractions. For each: name, file path, and a one-sentence description of what it does.\\n\\n### Flow\\nThe execution flow step by step. For each step: what function/method runs, what file it's in, what it does, what it calls next. Include the data that flows between steps.\\n\\n### Files Read\\nEvery file you read during exploration, so the explainer can reference them.\\n\\n### Boundaries\\nWhere this subsystem connects to other parts of the codebase. The inputs and outputs.\\n\\n### Non-Obvious Things\\nAnything surprising, historically motivated, or easy to get wrong. Things that look like they should work one way but work another.\\n\\n### Open Questions\\nAnything you couldn't fully trace or understand. Be honest about gaps.\\n# Explainer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild the explainer worker's prompt from this template. Fill in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are writing an architectural explanation for a senior engineer. For complex questions, explorers have traced different slices and gathered findings, independently when supported or sequentially with an explicit reduced-capability disclosure. For simple questions, omit Explorer Findings and explore the source yourself in this role. Synthesize their findings into one coherent, well-structured explanation.\\n\\n## Original Question\\n\\n> {QUESTION}\\n\\n## Explorer Findings\\n\\n{EXPLORER_FINDINGS_ALL}\\n\\n## Instructions\\n\\nThe explorers each investigated a different angle of the same subsystem. Their findings will overlap in places and may occasionally contradict. Reconcile them. Merge overlapping descriptions, resolve contradictions by checking the code yourself, and combine the separate slices into a unified picture.\\n\\nWrite an explanation a senior engineer unfamiliar with this area could read and walk away with a solid mental model, understanding the architecture well enough to start working in it confidently.\\n\\nYou have read-only access to the codebase to check anything, clarify a detail, or fill a gap. Use available file reading, symbol search and file discovery as needed. When explorer findings are supplied, you should not need to re-explore from scratch. Without them, trace entry points, calls, data, abstractions and boundaries yourself; disclose anything you cannot trace.\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nUse this structure, adapted to what makes sense for the question. Not every section is needed for every question.\\n\\n### Overview\\n1-2 paragraphs. What is this thing, what does it do, why does it exist. Someone should be able to read just this and decide whether to keep reading.\\n\\n### Key Concepts\\nThe important types, services, or abstractions needed to follow the rest. Brief definitions, not exhaustive.\\n\\n### How It Works\\nThe core of the explanation, and the longest section. Walk through the flow: what triggers it, what happens step by step, where data goes, what the decision points are.\\n\\nUse prose, not pseudocode. Reference specific files and functions so the reader knows where to look, but don't dump large code blocks unless a snippet is essential to a point.\\n\\nWhen the flow involves multiple components talking to each other, or data transforming through stages, include a diagram. Use mermaid (```mermaid) for structured flows (sequence diagrams, flowcharts, component graphs) or ASCII art for simpler relationships where mermaid would be overkill. Use your judgment. A diagram should clarify, not decorate. If prose covers the flow, skip the diagram.\\n\\n### Where Things Live\\nA brief file/directory map. Just the ones someone would need to start working here.\\n\\n### Gotchas\\nNon-obvious things, surprising behavior, historical context, pitfalls. Skip this section if there's nothing worth calling out.\\n\\n## Communication Style\\n\\n- Use concrete language, not abstractions-about-abstractions\\n- Say \\\"the `UserService` calls `AuthClient.refresh()`\\\" not \\\"the service delegates to the client\\\"\\n- When something is complex, explain why it's complex. Don't just describe the complexity\\n- When something is simple, don't pad it out\\n- If there's a helpful analogy, use it. If there isn't, don't force one\\n- If the explorers flagged open questions or gaps, acknowledge them rather than hiding them\\n---\\nname: why\\ndescription: \\\"Use for 'why does X work this way', 'why we picked Y', design rationale, regressions, postmortems, or data-backed thresholds. Discovers available MCPs and queries each evidence category (source control, issue tracker, long-form docs, real-time chat, infrastructure observability, error tracking, product analytics warehouse) in parallel, then returns a cited read on decisions and tradeoffs. Use how for runtime behavior.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Why\\n\\nInvestigate the motivation and intent behind code.\\n\\nCompanion to the `how` skill. `how` answers what the code does and how it works. `why` answers what forces led to its shape.\\n\\nRead [host capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model roles](../../references/model-roles.md), and the [orchestration contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md) before assigning investigators. Bind operations to tools exposed in this session and observe results. Preserve the role labels `why investigators` and `why synthesizer`; select supported models under the model-role contract. Record requested and actual models only when observed, otherwise use `unknown`. Invocation metadata above is retained without assuming host enforcement.\\n\\n## Operating Posture\\n\\nOperate as a **careful, cautious, and precise investigator**. Be honest about what you know vs what you're inferring. Read `references/epistemics.md` for the full confidence framework and phrasing guide. The synthesizer must follow it.\\n\\n## Step 1. Understand the Target and the Question\\n\\nParse what the user is asking. The **target** is usually a chunk of code, a pattern, a feature, or a named design decision. The **question** is usually a design rationale, a tradeoff, a motivating edge case, an external constraint, dead code, or a broad history sweep.\\n\\nIf the target is vague (\\\"why do we do it this way?\\\" with no clear referent), make your best guess from conversation context (open files, recent edits, cursor location, what was just discussed). State your interpretation briefly so the user can redirect if you're off, then proceed.\\n\\n## Step 2. Establish the Code Anchor\\n\\nBefore spawning investigators, anchor the investigation in concrete code. You need:\\n\\n- The relevant file path(s) and line range(s)\\n- The key symbols (function names, class names, constants)\\n- An initial commit list. The last few commits touching the target.\\n- PR numbers from merge commits (pattern `(#1234)` in the subject line)\\n\\nBuild this inline.\\n\\n```bash\\n# Blame target lines for last-touch commits\\ngit blame -L <start>,<end> <file>\\n\\n# Full file history, with patches, through renames\\ngit log --follow -p -- <file>\\n\\n# Last N commits touching the file, PR numbers visible\\ngit log --oneline -20 -- <file>\\n\\n# Extract PR numbers from a commit message\\ngit log -1 --format=%B <commit>\\n```\\n\\nPull PR bodies and discussion via `gh` for any substantive commits:\\n\\n```bash\\ngh pr view <number> --json title,body,author,createdAt,mergedAt,labels,closingIssuesReferences,comments,reviews\\n```\\n\\nCapture this as seed context (file paths, symbols, commits, PR numbers, linked ticket IDs). Pass it to the investigators.\\n\\n## Step 3. Spawn Parallel Investigators (default posture)\\n\\n**Default to the full parallel investigation.**\\n\\n### Discovery\\n\\nBefore spawning investigators, inspect the session’s exposed tools and connector descriptions. Do not assume a host-specific MCP directory exists. Discover read operations, authorization and observed access for each category. Source-specific API names in the playbooks are examples; bind their purpose to the actual tool schema before use.\\n\\nMap each available MCP to one evidence category:\\n\\n1. Source control history\\n2. Issue / ticket tracker\\n3. Long-form documents\\n4. Real-time team chat\\n5. Infrastructure observability\\n6. Error / exception tracking\\n7. Product analytics warehouse\\n\\nTry authorized local git history and available `gh` or source-control connector reads. Neither execution nor authenticated PR access is guaranteed. Keep local history and shared PR records distinct, and report missing access explicitly. For the other six, classify using the MCP name, se…968 tokens truncated…# When to skip an investigator\\n\\nOnly skip with an **explicit, written justification** that goes in the final \\\"Sources Consulted\\\" section. Two valid reasons:\\n\\n- **No MCP is available for that category** in this environment. Flag this as a gap, not a choice. Example: \\\"Real-time team chat skipped. No matching MCP available, so the conversational record was not searchable.\\\"\\n- **The source is provably irrelevant**, not just \\\"probably irrelevant.\\\" A high bar. Example: \\\"Error / exception tracking skipped. Target is a build-time script with no runtime code path.\\\"\\n\\nIf your scope assessment suggests a single-commit trivial target where the PR description already contains the complete answer, you may answer inline **only after** confirming all seven available category searches would be redundant. Say so explicitly. This should be rare.\\n\\n## Step 4. Synthesize\\n\\nSpawn one synthesizer through an observed worker binding, using the `why synthesizer` role and authorized source reads for citation checks. Use sequential synthesis with an explicit reduced-capability label when workers are unavailable. The synthesizer must not write files or modify external state.\\n\\nThe synthesizer gets:\\n1. The investigator findings, including any null results and any categories skipped with justification\\n2. The code anchor from Step 2 (file paths, symbols, commit hashes, PR numbers, ticket IDs)\\n3. The user's original question\\n4. The epistemics framework from `references/epistemics.md`\\n5. The synthesizer prompt template from `references/synthesizer-prompt.md`\\n\\n## Step 5. Present\\n\\nTake the synthesizer's output and present it to the user. You may lightly edit for clarity or add context from the conversation, but **do not rewrite the confidence language**.\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nThe output structure is the one in `references/synthesizer-prompt.md`: The Question, The Code in Question, What We Found, What We Can Reasonably Infer, Competing Hypotheses, What We Don't Know, Sources Consulted, Confidence Summary. Adapt as needed, but keep the confidence separation intact, and keep Sources Consulted as one line per investigator, including the ones that returned nothing or were skipped, with the reason.\\n\\nAfter the Sources Consulted block, if the user's `why` question is a precursor to actually changing this code, convert the lineage findings into a Preserve / Change / Avoid / Risk constraint set suitable for planning the change.\\n\\n## Common Failure Modes to Avoid\\n\\n- **Recency bias**. Assuming the most recent commit is authoritative. The current shape is often the accretion of many earlier decisions. Trace back.\\n\\nKeep private source visibility intact. Retrieve only authorized relevant content, avoid raw telemetry or personal-data dumps, and do not redistribute restricted records beyond the authorized audience. External messages and writes require separate explicit authorization.\\n\\n## Reference Files\\n\\n- `references/epistemics.md`. Confidence tiers and phrasing guide. The synthesizer must follow it.\\n- `references/investigator-prompt.md`. Base prompt template for investigator subagents.\\n- `references/source-playbook.md`. Index pointing at the category playbooks below.\\n- `references/sources/*.md`. One self-contained example playbook per category, plus cross-cutting `incident-postmortem.md`. Give an investigator the single file that matches its category and adapt it to the available MCP.\\n- `references/synthesizer-prompt.md`. Prompt template for the synthesizer subagent, including the output format.\\n# Investigator Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild each investigator's prompt from this template. Fill in the placeholders. Append the single category playbook `sources/<source>.md` matching this investigator's evidence category (see `source-playbook.md` for the index). If the target code looks defensive (null checks, retry logic, timeout handling, rate limiting, feature flags, egress guards, OOM handlers), also append `sources/incident-postmortem.md` for the incident-flavored queries to run inside its own source.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are investigating the historical context and motivation behind a piece of code. A separate synthesizer combines your findings with other investigators' into a final answer, so gather evidence accurately rather than writing prose.\\n\\nOther investigators search different sources in parallel. Don't try to cover everything. Focus on your assigned source and go deep.\\n\\n## Operating Posture\\n\\nWork like a careful, cautious, precise investigator. Don't produce a narrative. Surface evidence and describe it accurately, including the parts that don't fit a tidy story. The more boring and exact your output, the more useful it is. A single verbatim quote with a precise citation beats a paragraph of plausible-sounding summary.\\n\\n- **Quote, don't paraphrase** when the exact wording matters. Citations should let the reader jump to the source and confirm the claim in seconds.\\n- **Go wide before going deep.** Cast a broad first net so you don't miss related context. Only then narrow in.\\n- **Track what you searched, not just what you found.** An absence is only useful if the reader knows what was looked for. Record queries verbatim.\\n- **Resist the story.** If three pieces of evidence line up neatly and a fourth contradicts them, the contradiction is the most interesting finding. Don't file it away.\\n- **Consider the counterfactual.** Before reporting a finding as strong, ask whether you would expect to find it if your current reading were wrong, and how the evidence would differ.\\n- **Never invent.** If you're tempted to round a partial finding up into a confident statement, stop and label it partial. The synthesizer is counting on your output being accurate.\\n\\n## The Question\\n\\n> {QUESTION}\\n\\n## The Code Anchor\\n\\n**Target files:** {FILES_WITH_LINE_RANGES}\\n\\n**Key symbols:** {SYMBOLS}\\n\\n**Initial commits touching this code (most recent first):**\\n{COMMIT_LIST}\\n\\n**PR numbers extracted from commit messages:** {PR_NUMBERS}\\n\\n**Ticket IDs mentioned in commits or PR bodies (if any):** {TICKET_IDS}\\n\\n## Your Assigned Source\\n\\n{SOURCE_NAME}\\n\\n{SOURCE_PLAYBOOK_SECTION}\\n\\n## Investigation Instructions\\n\\nGather **evidence**. Don't answer the question directly. The synthesizer weighs the evidence and forms conclusions. Follow this loop:\\n\\n1. **Cast a wide net first.** Start broad so you don't miss related context, then narrow in on specific items.\\n2. **Read the whole thing.** Read any PR, ticket, doc, or thread fully, not just the title or summary. The key evidence is often buried in a comment, a subtask, or a follow-up.\\n3. **Follow links within your assigned source.** If a PR references another PR or commit, pull it. If a ticket links a parent or sibling, pull it. If a doc links another doc, pull it. Stay inside your assigned source. When you spot a cross-source reference, do NOT chase it yourself. Record it under \\\"Additional Leads\\\" so the investigator assigned to that source can pick it up. The one-investigator-per-category design depends on this. Chasing cross-source links duplicates work and confuses scope.\\n4. **Capture quotes verbatim** with their location (PR number, ticket ID, URL, commit hash, file:line). The synthesizer needs to cite this precisely.\\n5. **Note absences.** If you searched for something and came up empty, that's also a finding. Record what you searched for and what you didn't find.\\n6. **Watch for contradictions.** If two items in your source disagree, record both. Don't suppress the inconvenient one.\\n\\nDon't synthesize or form a final opinion on \\\"the why.\\\" Collect the raw material honestly and completely. The synthesizer does the reasoning.\\n\\n## Epistemic Discipline\\n\\n- **Don't confuse mechanics with motivation.** A commit changing `limit = 50` to `limit = 100` shows the change, not necessarily why. Look for the explanation in the commit message, PR description, linked ticket, or review comments.\\n- **Don't infer intent from code style.** \\\"The author chose a functional approach\\\" is an observation about code, not evidence of intent. Claim intent only when the author stated it.\\n- **Preserve uncertainty.** If the evidence is ambiguous, say so. If one reading is more plausible but not certain, say that. Don't collapse ambiguity to look decisive.\\n- **No silent substitutions.** If the question is about feature X and you only find evidence about feature Y, don't present Y's evidence as if it answers X.\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nReturn your findings in this structure. The synthesizer will read it directly.\\n\\n### Source\\nWhich source you investigated (source control, issue / ticket tracker, long-form documents, real-time team chat, infrastructure observability, error / exception tracking, product analytics warehouse, code comments, etc.).\\n\\n### What I Searched\\nThe queries you ran, the items you opened, the places you looked. Be specific. This tells the synthesizer how thorough the investigation was and what might still be unsearched.\\n\\n### Direct Evidence Found\\nFor each piece that explicitly addresses the question:\\n- **What it says**: verbatim quote or accurate paraphrase\\n- **Where it's from**: PR #123, ticket ID, doc URL, chat permalink, commit hash, or file:line\\n- **Author and date** (if available)\\n- **Relevance**: one sentence on how it bears on the question\\n\\n### Indirect / Circumstantial Evidence\\nItems that don't explicitly answer the question but bear on it. For each:\\n- **What it is**: brief description\\n- **Where it's from**: location\\n- **What it suggests**: what a careful reader might infer, and why. Name the inference chain.\\n- **Alternative readings**: if the same evidence could support a different interpretation, note it\\n\\n### Contradictions\\nTwo items that disagree with each other, with both citations.\\n\\n### Gaps\\nWhat you searched for and didn't find. Be specific: \\\"Searched the issue tracker for [query] across [time range]. No matching issues.\\\" These absences are valuable data.\\n\\n### Additional Leads\\nAnything that suggests further investigation in a different source. For example, if a PR references a chat thread that wasn't in your source, note it so the real-time team chat investigator or a follow-up pass can pursue it.\\n\\n## What You're Not Doing\\n\\n- Writing the final answer. The synthesizer does that.\\n- Picking sides in contradictions. Surface them.\\n- Speculating beyond what the evidence supports. A hunch with no evidence isn't evidence.\\n- Reading the code itself to figure out intent. You may read the code to understand what the target *is*, but don't confuse \\\"what the code does\\\" with \\\"why.\\\"\\n# Synthesizer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild the synthesizer's prompt from this template. Fill in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are answering a \\\"why\\\" question about a piece of code by synthesizing findings from multiple investigators who searched different historical sources (source control, issue / ticket tracker, long-form documents, real-time team chat, infrastructure observability, error / exception tracking, product analytics warehouse, and code comments). Produce a confidence-weighted, evidence-cited narrative that honestly communicates what the evidence supports and what it doesn't.\\n\\n## The Question\\n\\n> {QUESTION}\\n\\n## The Code Anchor\\n\\n**Target files:** {FILES_WITH_LINE_RANGES}\\n\\n**Key symbols:** {SYMBOLS}\\n\\n## Investigator Findings\\n\\n{ALL_INVESTIGATOR_FINDINGS}\\n\\n## Sources That Weren't Searched\\n\\n{SKIPPED_SOURCES_WITH_REASONS}\\n\\n## Epistemics Framework\\n\\nYou MUST follow the framework in `references/epistemics.md`. Read it in full before writing the output. The key rules:\\n\\n1. Every claim sits in one of these tiers: **Direct**, **Supported**, **Inferred**, **Speculative**, **Unknown**. The tier determines what section the claim goes in and how it's phrased.\\n2. Every Direct/Supported claim must have a citation (PR #, ticket ID, doc URL, chat permalink, commit hash, or file:line).\\n3. Inferred and Speculative claims must use hedged language (\\\"appears to\\\", \\\"likely\\\", \\\"suggests\\\", \\\"one possibility is\\\").\\n4. Never cite code as evidence for its own intent.\\n5. Gaps in the evidence must be documented. Don't fill them with plausible-sounding guesses.\\n6. If the user's question embedded a hypothesis, treat it as a candidate, not a conclusion. Check the evidence independently.\\n\\n## Instructions\\n\\n1. **Read all investigator findings.** They gathered raw evidence, not conclusions. You weigh it.\\n2. **Reconcile overlapping findings.** Multiple investigators may have cited the same PR, ticket, or doc. Merge into a single, authoritative reference.\\n3. **Identify contradictions.** If two items of evidence disagree, don't pick one. Surface both.\\n4. **Calibrate confidence.** For each claim, identify the evidence and the tier. State Direct claims plainly with a citation. Hedge Inferred claims and explain the inference. Mark Speculative claims explicitly. Put claims with no evidence in the gaps section.\\n5. **Verify citations by spot-checking.** You can read the codebase and call MCP tools to verify citations. Do not write files, commit, or modify external state. If you're uncertain a cited item exists or says what's claimed, check it. Don't propagate errors.\\n6. **Don't overreach.** The user will act on your output. Better to leave an open question open than to fill it with a confident-sounding guess.\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nWrite the output for the user. Use this exact structure:\\n\\n---\\n\\n### The Question\\n\\nRestate the user's question in one or two sentences so the answer is anchored.\\n\\n### The Code in Question\\n\\nFile paths, line ranges, key symbols. Two or three lines to orient a reader who lands here cold.\\n\\n### What We Found\\n\\n**Claims with direct evidence**, one per bullet. Quote or paraphrase the source and cite precisely. Format each finding like:\\n\\n```markdown\\n- **[Direct]** {Claim}. Source: [PR #123](url) / ticket ID / file:line. {Brief quote or paraphrase.}\\n- **[Supported]** {Claim}. Evidence: {list of items and what each contributes}.\\n```\\n\\nUse `[Direct]` for single-source, explicit evidence. Use `[Supported]` when multiple indirect items converge on a conclusion.\\n\\n### What We Can Reasonably Infer\\n\\n**Claims that aren't explicitly stated anywhere but are well-supported by indirect evidence.** Make the inference chain visible: \\\"Given A and B, it's likely that C.\\\" Use hedged language (\\\"appears to\\\", \\\"likely\\\", \\\"suggests\\\", \\\"is consistent with\\\"). Format:\\n\\n- **[Inferred]** {Hedged claim}. Reasoning: {the specific evidence and the inference step}.\\n\\nIf there's nothing to infer, skip this section.\\n\\n### Competing Hypotheses\\n\\n**If the evidence fits multiple stories, present them.** Don't force a winner when the record doesn't support one. For each hypothesis:\\n\\n- **Hypothesis:** {one-sentence statement}\\n- **Evidence for:** {specific items}\\n- **Evidence against or missing:** {what would need to be true but isn't, or what counter-signals exist}\\n\\nSkip this section if there's a single clear answer.\\n\\n### What We Don't Know\\n\\n**Explicit gaps.** Things the user asked that the evidence didn't answer. Sources searched that came up empty. Sources that weren't searchable at all, such as a missing real-time team chat MCP.\\n\\nBe specific. \\\"We searched the issue tracker for [query1], [query2], [query3] and found no issue discussing the rate-limit threshold\\\" is useful. \\\"We don't know why\\\" is not. Include:\\n\\n- Specific questions that went unanswered\\n- Searches that returned nothing\\n- Sources that were unavailable (and why)\\n- People who would likely know but who you can't ask\\n\\n### Sources Consulted\\n\\nBulleted list of what was actually searched, so the user can judge coverage and redirect. Format:\\n\\n- **Source control history**: {file paths}, {number of commits reviewed}, PRs #{numbers}, and code comments searched. Or \\\"Not searched. Git history, command execution or authenticated PR access was unavailable or denied; name the exact gap.\\\"\\n- **Issue / ticket tracker**: {ticket IDs and keyword searches}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Long-form documents**: {page titles and search queries}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Real-time team chat**: {channels searched, date ranges, queries}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Infrastructure observability**: {dashboards, monitors, metrics, logs, traces, or incidents searched}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Error / exception tracking**: {issues, events, or releases searched}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Product analytics warehouse**: {fully-qualified tables queried, the time windows, and the numeric summaries (counts, percentiles, first/last-seen timestamps) that bore on the question}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n\\n### Confidence Summary\\n\\nOne or two sentences summarizing your overall confidence. E.g.:\\n\\n> \\\"The core rationale (A) is well-supported by direct PR and ticket evidence. The specific threshold value (100) is inferred from the surrounding context but not explicitly documented. The question of whether this was driven by a customer request could not be answered. No relevant issue tracker or long-form doc content surfaced, and real-time team chat search was unavailable.\\\"\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Quality Check Before Returning\\n\\nBefore finalizing, review your output against this checklist:\\n\\n1. Does every claim in \\\"What We Found\\\" have a citation? If not, add one or move the claim to \\\"Inferred\\\" or \\\"Hypotheses.\\\"\\n2. Is the phrasing tier-appropriate? (Direct claims can use \\\"because\\\". Inferred claims cannot.)\\n3. Did you surface any contradictions you noticed, or did you quietly pick one?\\n4. Does the \\\"What We Don't Know\\\" section exist and name specific gaps? If it's empty or missing, be suspicious. Historical investigations almost always have gaps.\\n5. If the user embedded a hypothesis in their question, did you check it against the evidence rather than rubber-stamping it?\\n6. Did you cite any code as evidence for its own intent? Remove those. Code is mechanics, not motivation.\\n7. Is the overall tone calibrated? A confident-sounding answer with weak evidence is the exact failure mode this skill exists to prevent.\\n\\nIf any item fails, revise before returning.\\n\\n## A Final Note\\n\\nThe value of this output comes from its honesty, not its authority. A reader who takes your answer to the original author, an engineering lead, or a product manager should be well-positioned to ask the right follow-up questions. Be clear about what's known, what's inferred, and what's missing. Don't optimize for looking decisive. Optimize for being useful.\\n---\\nname: unslop\\ndescription: Cut AI tells from any writing. Must always apply.\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Unslop\\n\\nEdit text to remove AI patterns.\\n\\n## Process\\n\\n1. Scan for the patterns below.\\n2. Rewrite. Preserve meaning, match intended tone.\\n\\n## Patterns to detect and fix\\n\\nRule numbers are stable ids that other skills cite. A removed rule leaves a gap.\\n\\n### Content\\n\\n3. **Superficial -ing phrases.** \\\"highlighting...\\\", \\\"ensuring...\\\", \\\"reflecting...\\\", \\\"showcasing...\\\", \\\"fostering...\\\". Delete or expand with real sources.\\n5. **Vague attributions.** \\\"Experts believe\\\", \\\"Industry reports suggest\\\", \\\"Some critics argue\\\". Name the source or delete.\\n\\n### Language\\n\\n7. **AI vocabulary.** Additionally, crucial, delve, enduring, enhance, fostering, garner, interplay, intricate, landscape (abstract), pivotal, showcase, tapestry (abstract), testament, underscore, vibrant. Replace with plain words.\\n8. **Fancy ways to say \\\"is\\\".** \\\"serves as\\\", \\\"stands as\\\", \\\"boasts\\\", \\\"features\\\". Just say \\\"is\\\" or \\\"has\\\".\\n9. **\\\"Not just X, but Y.\\\"** State the point directly instead.\\n10. **Rule of three.** Forcing ideas into groups of three. Use the natural number.\\n11. **Synonym cycling.** Protagonist, main character, central figure, hero all in one paragraph. Pick one, repeat it.\\n12. **False ranges.** \\\"from X to Y\\\" where X and Y aren't on a meaningful scale. List topics directly.\\n\\n### Style\\n\\n13. **Em dash overuse.** Avoid em dashes entirely. Use periods or commas only (no parentheses, no en dashes, no hyphen-as-dash substitutes). If a thought needs separation, end the sentence or use a comma.\\n14. **Colon overuse.** Colons are fine before a list or example. Not as mid-sentence connectors. \\\"If you're coming from traditional automation: instead of registering event handlers, you describe conditions\\\" adds nothing with the colon. Rewrite to let the point stand on its own without comparison framing. \\\"Describing when the scheduler should fire works best as plain English.\\\" Same meaning, no crutch punctuation.\\n15. **Boldface overuse.** Don't bold every proper noun or acronym.\\n16. **Inline-header lists.** The tell is a bold label and colon that restates the line: \\\"**Performance:** Performance improved...\\\". Convert those to prose. A bold lead-in that ends in a period, names the item, and is followed by genuinely new detail (\\\"**Schema in TypeScript.** Tables live in one file.\\\") is fine, not a tell.\\n17. **Title case headings.** Use sentence case.\\n18. **Decorative emojis.** Remove from headings and bullets.\\n19. **Curly quotes.** Replace with straight quotes.\\n\\n### Communication artifacts\\n\\n20. **Chatbot phrases.** \\\"I hope this helps!\\\", \\\"Let me know if...\\\", \\\"Of course!\\\", \\\"Certainly!\\\", \\\"Found the smoking gun!\\\" Remove.\\n22. **Sycophantic tone.** \\\"Great question! You're absolutely right!\\\" Respond directly.\\n\\n### Filler\\n\\n23. **Filler phrases.** \\\"In order to\\\" becomes \\\"To\\\". \\\"Due to the fact that\\\" becomes \\\"Because\\\". \\\"It is important to note that\\\" gets deleted.\\n24. **Excessive hedging.** \\\"could potentially possibly be argued that it might\\\" becomes \\\"may\\\".\\n25. **Generic conclusions.** \\\"The future looks bright.\\\" State specific plans or facts.\\n\\n### Jargon\\n\\n26. **Abstract metaphor nouns.** Substrate, wedge, vector, locus, vantage, nexus, primitive (as noun), harness (as metaphor), surface (as in \\\"API surface\\\"), bedrock, scaffolding (as metaphor), modality, paradigm, gold-plating, ratchet (as metaphor), evacuate (for moving code), endgame, north star, flywheel. These read as technical but usually have a plainer concrete word. \\\"Substrate\\\" becomes \\\"base\\\". \\\"Wedge in\\\" becomes \\\"add\\\". \\\"Vector\\\" becomes \\\"way\\\" or \\\"method\\\". \\\"Gold-plating\\\" becomes \\\"more than the job needs\\\". \\\"Ratchet\\\" becomes the mechanism's real name or \\\"a limit that only tightens\\\". \\\"Evacuate\\\" becomes \\\"move out\\\". \\\"Endgame\\\" becomes \\\"the last phase\\\". Pick the concrete word.\\n\\n### Plain speech\\n\\n27. **Say what it does, not how it feels.** \\\"the database stays close at hand\\\", \\\"SQL you can read\\\", \\\"types that follow your schema\\\" name a feeling. The fix names the mechanism or a number: \\\"`.toSQL()` returns the exact string sent to the database\\\", \\\"a column rename fails the build\\\". Ask what the sentence tells the reader to do or know, then write that. If you can't restate it as a concrete instruction, fact, or number, cut it. One more check: if the sentence could appear unchanged in another project's docs, it says nothing about this one. Cut it.\\n28. **Shorten or split dense sentences.** If the reader has to backtrack to parse a sentence, break it in two or drop clauses. One idea per sentence.\\n29. **Active voice.** Prefer it. Catch \\\"is/are/was/were + past participle\\\" and name the actor: \\\"queries are validated\\\" becomes \\\"the compiler validates queries\\\", \\\"the file is parsed by the loader\\\" becomes \\\"the loader parses the file\\\". Passive is fine only when the actor is unknown or genuinely doesn't matter.\\n30. **Cut adverbs, or use a stronger verb.** \\\"runs quickly\\\" becomes \\\"is fast\\\" or the number. \\\"significantly improves\\\" becomes the measured delta. An adverb propping up a weak verb means the verb is wrong.\\n31. **Prefer the plain word.** \\\"utilize\\\" becomes \\\"use\\\", \\\"leverage\\\" becomes \\\"use\\\", \\\"facilitate\\\" becomes \\\"help\\\", \\\"numerous\\\" becomes \\\"many\\\", \\\"in the event that\\\" becomes \\\"if\\\". The fancier synonym is rarely clearer.\\n32. **Mannered prose.** Metaphor or flourish where a literal phrase exists: aphorisms (\\\"wire it or delete it\\\"), rhetorical fragments for effect, personified code (\\\"the plan holds it\\\"), figurative verbs (\\\"rides along\\\", \\\"stands on\\\"), stock framing phrases. \\\"A dial worth turning\\\" becomes \\\"a parameter worth varying\\\". Say what you mean. Rule 26 covers the metaphor nouns.\\n33. **Over-compression.** Dropped articles, verbless fragments, symbol-speak, and abbreviations that make the reader decode instead of read. \\\"Parser rejects bad date → exit 2, no write\\\" becomes \\\"The parser rejects a bad date, exits with code 2, and writes nothing.\\\" Write whole sentences with their articles and verbs, and spell out arrows and abbreviations.\\n---\\nname: principle-separate-before-serializing-shared-state\\ndescription: \\\"Apply when concurrent actors might write to the same file, branch, key, or state object. Eliminate the sharing first; serialize structurally only when one shared writer is a real invariant.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Separate Before Serializing Shared State\\n\\nWhen concurrent actors might share mutable state, first ask whether they need the same mutable object. If not, eliminate the sharing. When sharing is real, enforce serialization structurally: lockfiles, sequential phases, exclusive ownership. Instructions and conventions are not concurrency control.\\n\\n**Why:** Concurrent writes to shared state create race conditions that are intermittent, hard to reproduce, and expensive to debug.\\n\\n**Pattern:**\\n1. **Identify shared mutable state** (files both read and write, branches both push to, APIs both define and consume).\\n2. **Default: eliminate the shared write target.** Ask: do these actors need one canonical object, or are they publishing independent facts? Give each actor its own owned file, key, branch, or state directory, and merge only at the read/reporting boundary. Two workers writing their own `lastX` field into one `state.json` is still shared mutation. `indexer-state.json` + `metrics-state.json` is not.\\n3. **Only when one shared write target is a real invariant, serialize access structurally** (lockfiles, sequential phases, single-writer actor, or atomic compare-and-swap). Treat \\\"we need a lock\\\" as a design smell to check, not as the default answer.\\n\\n## Session capabilities and permissions\\n\\nBefore dispatching writers or changing shared state, check [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md) and follow the [assignment and integration contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md). Assign distinct owned paths before writes start. Isolation does not grant permission to spawn workers, write files or perform external actions.\\n\\nIf execution or writes are unavailable or denied, state the missing requirement and provide the isolation design without claiming implementation. Authorized sequential work is reduced capability. A design or sequential run does not prove independent workers or prevention of concurrent races.\\n---\\nname: principle-laziness-protocol\\ndescription: \\\"Apply when refactoring, evaluating diff size, or tempted to add abstractions, layers, or signal threading. Bias toward deletion and the smallest change that solves the problem.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Laziness Protocol\\n\\nAim for the most result with the least code and complexity.\\n\\n- **Prefer deletion.** When asked to refactor or improve, look for removals before additions.\\n- **Maintain a flat call hierarchy.** Avoid deep call chains. A rich interface that hides substantial work is not a deep call chain. If answering a question requires tracing through more than 3 files or layers, flatten it.\\n- **Consolidate decisions.** Do not repeat the same choice in several places. Put it behind one source of truth and pass the result as a simple flag.\\n- **Minimize the diff.** Make the smallest change that solves the problem. Fewer lines beat \\\"elegant\\\" boilerplate.\\n- **Question the threading.** If a task asks you to pass a new signal through types, schemas, pipelines, or similar layers, stop and look for a more direct path.\\n- **Sweat the small leaks.** Remove tiny pass-throughs, representation leaks, and duplicated choices before they spread. Small leaks compound into permanent coordination costs.\\n\\n**The test:** If a human developer would find the code exhausting to maintain, it is a bad solution.\\n\\n## Capability limits\\n\\nApply this principle within the authorized task. Follow [session capability checks](../../references/host-capabilities.md) before claiming file reads, edits or verification. When writes or execution are unavailable, label the read-only proposal as reduced capability and state the missing checks. If required source is unavailable, state that the refactor cannot be assessed. Structural validation does not prove runtime behavior.\\n---\\nname: principle-prove-it-works\\ndescription: \\\"Apply after completing a task, before declaring done. Verify against the real artifact (run the feature, read the actual value, inspect the diff), not a proxy, self-report, or 'it compiles.'\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Prove It Works\\n\\nVerify every task output by checking the real thing directly. Do not infer from proxies, self-reports, or \\\"it compiles.\\\"\\n\\n**Why:** Unverified work has unknown correctness. Indirect verification (file mtimes, output freshness, agent self-reports, cached screenshots) feels cheaper than direct observation. Acting on a wrong inference costs far more than checking the source.\\n\\nCheck the real thing, not a proxy:\\n- Check process liveness directly, not indirectly through derived state\\n- Read the actual value, not a cached or derived representation\\n- When verification fails, suspect the observation method before suspecting the system\\n\\n## Script the check when you can\\n\\nThe strongest proof is a deterministic script that re-runs the same comparison, not a one-time eyeball. Write the script, run it, and keep its output as an artifact a reviewer can re-run instead of trusting your word.\\n\\nKeep the artifact visible for the human. Commit it only for large or complex work where the trail has to be auditable later, like a big port or migration (the **show-me-your-work** skill).\\n\\n## Session capabilities and permissions\\n\\nUse the exposed tools and observed operations described in [host capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md). Run checks and retain artifacts only within the user's authorized scope and host permissions. If direct inspection or execution is unavailable or denied, state which outputs remain unverified and withhold completion for those outputs. A proposed script is not evidence that it ran.\\n---\\nname: principle-redesign-from-first-principles\\ndescription: \\\"Apply when integrating a new requirement into an existing design. Redesign as if the requirement had been a foundational assumption from day one, instead of bolting it on.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Redesign From First Principles\\n\\nWhen integrating a change, don't bolt it onto the existing design. Redesign as if the requirement had been there from the start.\\n\\n- Read all affected files and understand the current design\\n- Ask: \\\"if we were writing this from scratch with this new requirement, what would we build?\\\"\\n- Propagate the change through every reference: types, docs, examples, rationale sections\\n- Think about the whole redesign, then deliver it incrementally\\n\\nThis is the method for preserving option value when integrating changes into an existing design.\\n\\n## Capability and permission boundaries\\n\\nUse the [session capability contract](../../references/host-capabilities.md) before reading source, editing files or running checks. Apply the redesign within the authorized task. Propagating references does not grant permission to change unrelated files or perform external actions.\\n\\nIf source is available but writes or execution are unavailable, provide a concrete redesign and incremental sequence as reduced capability. State which edits and checks were not performed. If affected source or design context is missing, identify the missing inputs and keep any redesign provisional. Claim implementation or verification only after observing the authorized operation.\\n\"\n  }\n}\n"

Names and assertions often encode the edge cases that motivated a change\\n- Related files modified in the same commits (co-change signal)\\n- CHANGELOG entries, release notes in the repo\\n- Issue/ticket IDs mentioned in commit messages and PR bodies\\n\\nThe most trustworthy source, tied directly to the code, and the most complete. Everything that went through the repo should be here.\\n\\n## How to search it\\n\\nExpand the seed commit list:\\n\\n```bash\\n# Full history of the file through renames\\ngit log --follow --oneline -- <file>\\n\\n# Pickaxe: commits that added or removed this exact text\\ngit log -S '<exact_string_from_code>' -- <file>\\n\\n# Or for patterns:\\ngit log -G '<regex>' -- <file>\\n\\n# Who wrote each line and when\\ngit blame -L <start>,<end> <file>\\n\\n# The full diff of a specific commit\\ngit show <hash>\\n\\n# Commits between two points affecting this file\\ngit log <old>..<new> -p -- <file>\\n```\\n\\nFor each substantive commit, pull the PR context:\\n\\n```bash\\n# Find the PR number from the merge commit or branch\\ngit log -1 --format=%B <hash>\\n\\n# Full PR context: body, review comments, linked issues\\ngh pr view <number> --json title,body,author,createdAt,mergedAt,labels,closingIssuesReferences,comments,reviews,files\\n\\n# The --json reviews and comments fields are where the real signal is\\n```\\n\\nLook for out-of-band docs:\\n\\n```bash\\n# ADRs often live in docs/adr/ or similar\\nrg -l -i 'architecture.decision' --glob '*.md'\\n\\n# TODOs and FIXMEs near the target\\nrg -n -C2 '(TODO|FIXME|HACK|XXX|NOTE)' <target_file>\\n\\n# Related tests. Names often encode the \\\"why\\\"\\nrg -l '<symbol>' --glob '*test*'\\n```\\n\\n## What good evidence looks like here\\n\\n- A PR description that explains the problem being solved, not just the change (\\\"This fixes the pagination bug that caused X\\\")\\n- A long review thread where alternatives were debated\\n- An inline comment near the target line that explains a non-obvious constraint\\n- A test named `test_handles_edge_case_when_X` that reveals an edge case motivating the code\\n- A commit message that references a ticket or incident ID\\n- A CHANGELOG entry that summarizes the user-visible rationale\\n\\n## Common pitfalls\\n\\n- **Squash-merge flatlands.** If the repo squashes PRs, individual commits in the branch history are lost. Fall back to PR body and comments.\\n- **Misleading commit messages.** \\\"Small refactor\\\" sometimes hides an intentional behavior change. Look at the diff, not the message.\\n- **Cargo-culted patterns.** The author may have copied a pattern without understanding why. Check if the pattern originated earlier in the codebase and investigate *that* commit.\\n- **Bot commits and auto-merges.** Dependabot, Renovate, and automated backports usually don't carry motivation. Skip them when trying to find intent.\\n- **Treating code as evidence of intent.** The code itself isn't evidence for why it exists. Evidence comes from commit messages, PRs, comments, tests, docs. Don't cite \\\"the function is named X\\\" as evidence of intent.\\n\\n## What to return\\n\\nEvery commit/PR/comment that bears on the question, with:\\n- The exact text (quoted)\\n- The hash / PR number / file:line\\n- Author and date\\n- Whether it's direct (explicitly addresses the question) or circumstantial\\n\\n## Session binding and access\\n\\nThese tool names and command examples describe source-specific operations, not guaranteed APIs. Inspect exposed schemas and bind equivalent authorized read operations before use. If execution, authentication, retention or source access is missing, record the precise gap. Do not install tools, change credentials, send messages or modify source state. Respect private-record visibility and return only relevant authorized excerpts or compact aggregates.\\n# Incident & Postmortem Context\\n\\nNot a separate source, a **cross-cutting angle**. Incidents often motivate defensive code (\\\"we added this check after the X outage\\\"), so if the target looks defensive (null checks, retry logic, timeout handling, rate limiting, feature flags), specifically hunt for incident history across every available source:\\n\\n- **Notion**: search for postmortems mentioning the target file, feature, or error string\\n- **Linear**: look for tickets labeled `incident`, `sev-*`, `postmortem-action-item`, `reliability`\\n- **Slack**: search `#sev-*` and `#incident-*` channels around the dates the target code was added\\n- **Git**: commits with messages like \\\"fix for incident\\\", \\\"add defensive check\\\", \\\"revert\\\" followed by \\\"re-apply with...\\\" are strong signals\\n- **Datadog**: `search_datadog_incidents` for formal incident records with timelines, dashboards and monitors created as postmortem action items\\n- **Sentry**: issues whose first-seen/last-seen window aligns with the target's PR ship date, stack traces through the target\\n- **Databricks**: product-analytics events that classify an error condition (client-reported failures, user-visible retry events, etc.) often spike during an incident window. A drop in that event count after the target PR ships is circumstantial support that the target code resolved the user-visible symptom, even when Datadog/Sentry signal is noisy.\\n\\nIf you find an incident link, fetch the full postmortem. Postmortems typically have an \\\"Action Items\\\" section that ties directly to code changes. When multiple sources corroborate (a Datadog incident ID appears in a Linear ticket, which appears in a Notion postmortem, which appears in a Slack thread that links to the target PR, and the Databricks error-event count drops after the fix), the evidence is especially strong.\\n\\nWorth spending time on when the code's defensive character makes an incident-driven origin plausible. Skip it for code that doesn't look defensive.\\n\\n## Session binding and access\\n\\nThese tool names and command examples describe source-specific operations, not guaranteed APIs. Inspect exposed schemas and bind equivalent authorized read operations before use. If execution, authentication, retention or source access is missing, record the precise gap. Do not install tools, change credentials, send messages or modify source state. Respect private-record visibility and return only relevant authorized excerpts or compact aggregates.\\n---\\nname: typescript-best-practices\\ndescription: TypeScript best practices. Use when reading or editing any .ts or .tsx file.\\npaths: [\\\"**/*.ts\\\", \\\"**/*.tsx\\\"]\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# TypeScript best practices\\n\\nApply the **type-system-discipline** principle skill first.\\n\\n| Rule | Summary |\\n|------|---------|\\n| Discriminated unions | Model variants with a `kind` literal discriminant so impossible states can't be represented. No optional-field bags. |\\n| Branded types | Brand primitives with `& { readonly __brand: \\\"X\\\" }` so they can't be mixed up. Validate once at the boundary. |\\n| Constructive modeling | Build the shape so the illegal value can't be constructed. `[T, ...T[]]` for non-empty, `[T, T][]` for even length, `start` plus `duration` for a range. Not a runtime guard, not a wish for refinement types. |\\n| Simplest total type | Keep `T[]` while every operation on it stays total. Strengthen to `NonEmpty<T>` only where the loose type forces `!`, a cast, or a \\\"should never happen\\\" throw. |\\n| `unknown` over `any` | External data is `unknown`. |\\n| Schemas before guards | Before hand-writing a property-by-property type guard, use the repository's runtime schema library and infer the type from the schema, such as `z.infer`. |\\n| No `as` casts | Every `as` is a runtime crash waiting. Cast only after validation. |\\n| Narrowing hierarchy | Discriminant switch > `in` operator > `typeof`/`instanceof` > user-defined type guard > `as`. |\\n| Type guards | Must verify the claim. A lying guard is worse than `as` because the bug hides behind a name that says it's safe. Name them `isX` or `hasX`. |\\n| Exhaustiveness | Inline `const _exhaustive: never = x;` in default arms so the compiler errors when a new variant is added. |\\n| `satisfies` over `as` | Validates the value without widening literal types. |\\n| Boundary validation | Parse where data crosses in, into a named domain type. `Record<string, unknown>` (however spelled) stops at that parse. Trust types inside. See the **boundary-discipline** principle skill. |\\n| Schema-derived types | Reach for `Pick`/`Omit`/`Parameters`/`ReturnType`/`Awaited`/`typeof` before declaring a new interface. |\\n| Object args | Pass objects, not positional, so argument order is self-documenting. Skip on hot paths (per-frame render, tokenizers, parsers). |\\n| Real tests | Don't mock what you can run. Prefer the framework's real test primitives with leak/disposable checks, and verify UI in a running build. Mock only what you can't run locally. |\\n| Structured telemetry | Prefer structured logger diagnostics with enough context to debug from an id. No `console.log` in shipped code. |\\n\\nExamples: `references/patterns.md`.\\n\\n## Session capabilities and permissions\\n\\nRead [host capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md) for the current session. If a dependency cannot be read, name it and label the review partial.\\n\\nApply these practices to supplied code or readable repository files. Saved edits require available write tools and authorization for the affected paths. If writes are denied or unavailable, return a proposed refactor in the conversation and state that it is unsaved. If execution is denied or unavailable, identify the checks that remain unperformed. No transport decisions. */\\nexport function isOrder(value: unknown): value is Order {\\n  return typeof value === \\\"object\\\" && value !== null\\n    && \\\"quantity\\\" in value && typeof value.quantity === \\\"number\\\"\\n    && Number.isInteger(value.quantity) && value.quantity > 0\\n    && \\\"unitPrice\\\" in value && typeof value.unitPrice === \\\"number\\\"\\n    && Number.isInteger(value.unitPrice) && value.unitPrice >= 0;\\n}\\n\\n/** Pure arithmetic for domain input already accepted at its boundary. */\\nexport function total(order: Order): number {\\n  return order.quantity * order.unitPrice;\\n}\\nimport { isOrder, total } from \\\"../domain/order\\\";\\n\\nexport function handle(raw: string): { status: number; body: string } {\\n  let order: unknown;\\n  try {\\n    order = JSON.parse(raw);\\n  } catch {\\n    return { status: 400, body: \\\"invalid json\\\" };\\n  }\\n  if (!isOrder(order)) return { status: 400, body: \\\"invalid order\\\" };\\n  return { status: 200, body: JSON.stringify({ total: total(order) }) };\\n}\\nimport { runBatch, type Order } from \\\"./index\\\";\\n\\nexport function invoiceTotals(): number[] {\\n  const orders: Order[] = [\\n    { quantity: 2, unitPrice: 125 },\\n    { quantity: 1, unitPrice: 99 },\\n  ];\\n  return runBatch(orders);\\n}\\nexport { handle } from \\\"./adapters/http\\\";\\nexport { runBatch } Keep show-me-your-work's Attention section when a trail was produced.\\n---\\nname: principle-model-the-domain\\ndescription: \\\"Apply when writing stateful logic, or when code branches a lot or repeats a shape assumption across files. Encode the domain in a structure instead of scattered conditionals.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Model the Domain\\n\\nEncode the real domain in a data structure instead of scattering it across conditionals.\\n\\n**Why:** Scattered booleans, repeated shape assumptions, and branching spread across files are accidental complexity. A structure that matches the domain makes invalid states unrepresentable and deletes branches. Choosing it at write time is cheap. Recovering it later reads as a refactor and gets deferred.\\n\\n**Reach for structures like these:**\\n\\n- A state machine instead of scattered booleans, phases, or lifecycle checks.\\n- A typed object/model instead of loose parameters or repeated shape assumptions.\\n- A map, registry, lookup table, or discriminated union instead of branching spread across files.\\n- A reducer or command/event model instead of ad hoc state mutations.\\n- A module organized around one body of domain knowledge instead of a sequence such as load, validate, transform, and save. Execution order is not ownership.\\n- A small module boundary that gathers repeated behavior, ownership, or invariants.\\n- A queue, cache, index, graph/tree, or normalized collection where the data access pattern calls for it.\\n- Any other structure that fits. When none fits, work out what the code must never allow and how the data gets read, then find the structure that encodes exactly that.\\n\\nDo not force an abstraction. Prefer boring code if the current shape is already clear, local, and unlikely to grow. Be skeptical of an abstraction that adds indirection without removing branches, duplicated rules, invalid states, or lifecycle risk.\\n\\nThe sign that you skipped this is a new feature that grows an existing if/else chain by one more branch, or a second boolean that must stay in sync with the first. Temporal decomposition is another sign. Phase-named modules repeat the same domain rules across steps.\\n\\n## Session capabilities and authorization\\n\\nFollow [session capability discovery](../../references/host-capabilities.md) for source reads, writes and execution. Apply the principle within the authorized task. If writes or execution are unavailable, provide a reduced design proposal and state which implementation checks remain unperformed. If required domain rules or source are missing, identify them and keep the design provisional. Claim implementation or verification only for observed operations.\\n\\nPreserve explicit-invocation metadata without assuming the host enforces it.\\n---\\nname: principle-test-behavior-not-implementation\\ndescription: \\\"Apply when you write, change, or keep a test. Call the code the way its users do and assert the result they observe against a literal expected value. If the test would still pass when every imported function returns undefined, rewrite the assertion or delete the test.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Test Behavior, Not Implementation\\n\\nA test calls the code the way its users do and asserts the result they observe against a literal expected value. A test that asserts which calls the code made, or restates a constant the code contains, does neither.\\n\\nThe check: before you keep a test, ask whether it would still pass if every function it imports returned `undefined`. If yes, it observes no behavior and cannot fail for a defect. Rewrite the assertion or delete the test.\\n\\n**Why:** A test that cannot fail for a defect costs CI time and review attention and catches nothing. A constant pin also fails when someone edits the constant or the prompt it restates, so it prevents that edit.\\n\\n**Five shapes that still pass when every imported function returns `undefined`:**\\n\\n- **Weak or no assertion.** No `expect`, or only `toBeDefined`, `toBeTruthy`, `not.toThrow`, `toBeInstanceOf`, `toBeGreaterThan(0)`.\\n- **Mock or absence only.** Only `toHaveBeenCalled`, `not.toHaveBeenCalled`, `toBeUndefined`, `toEqual([])`, `toHaveLength(0)`, `not.toBe(wrongValue)`.\\n- **Self-referential.** The expected value comes from the code under test: `expect(f(a)).toBe(f(a))`, `expect(parsed.url).toBe(buildUrl(...))`.\\n- **Constant pin.** The assertion restates a hand-maintained constant, config default, table row, or prompt string: `expect(LIMITS.maxTools).toBe(8)`, `expect(PROMPT).toContain(\\\"You are\\\")`.\\n- **Fixture asserts fixture.** The assertion reads data the test built or a value computed in `beforeEach`, and the subject never runs inside the body.\\n\\n**The fix:** call the subject inside the test body with one concrete input and assert the literal output or the observable effect, `expect(slugify(\\\"Hello, World!\\\")).toBe(\\\"hello-world\\\")`. For an absence, assert the presence on the other input in the same test. For a constant, test the mechanism that reads it with one input instead of restating the value. For a mock, assert the payload it received or the state after the call, not that it was called. When no such assertion exists, delete the test.\\n\\n**Keep** a test of a relation across a table's rows (a key present in two tables, a parent that exists), and a compile-time check in a `*.test-d.ts` file.\\n\\n## Capability and permission limits\\n\\nUse [the session capability contract](../../references/host-capabilities.md) when inspecting, changing or running tests. Work within authorized paths and permissions. If execution is unavailable or denied, provide the assertion and explain the expected empty-function failure, but report execution as unverified. Preserve `disable-model-invocation` metadata without assuming the host enforces it.\\n---\\nname: principle-foundational-thinking\\ndescription: \\\"Apply before writing logic: choosing core types and data structures, sequencing scaffold-vs-feature work, asking what concurrent actors share. Get the data structures right so downstream code becomes obvious.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Foundational Thinking\\n\\n**Structural decisions** protect option value. **Code-level decisions** protect simplicity.\\n\\n**Data structures first.** Get the data shape right before writing logic. Define core types early, trace every access pattern, and choose structures that match the dominant paths.\\n\\nAt code level, DRY the structure, not every line. Types and data models should converge. Three similar statements still beat a premature abstraction. Prefer explicit over clever. Test behavior and edge cases, not line counts.\\n\\n**Concurrency corollary.** Before sharing state between actors, ask \\\"what happens if another actor modifies this concurrently?\\\" If not \\\"nothing\\\", isolate.\\n\\n**Scaffold first.** If something helps every later phase, do it first. Ask \\\"does every subsequent phase benefit from this existing?\\\" CI, linting, test infrastructure, and shared types are scaffold. Sequence for option value: setup before features, tests before fixes. Keep commits small and single-purpose.\\n\\nEach increment should land a coherent abstraction or deepen one that exists. Do not spread a new capability across callers as special-case coordination.\\n\\nSubtraction comes before scaffolding. Remove dead code first, then lay foundations.\\n\\n## Capability and permission boundaries\\n\\nUse the [session capability contract](../../references/host-capabilities.md) before inspecting source, editing files or running checks. Apply this principle within the authorized task. A recommendation to set up CI or make a commit grants no permission to perform it.\\n\\nIf source is available but writes or execution are unavailable, give a concrete design and work sequence as reduced capability. State which edits and checks were not performed. If required source or access patterns are missing, identify them and keep any proposed shape provisional. Claim implementation or verification only after observing the authorized operation.\\n\"\n  }\n}\n"
