Do not run `origin pr merge` or `gh pr merge` unless the user explicitly asked to merge, land, ship, or merge when ready. Route that request to `playbooks/shipping.md`. A stacked PR whose parent has no required checks may merge immediately into that parent when merge-when-ready is armed. This collapses review granularity. A lost-ref race can also mark it merged without updating the parent ref.\\n\\n   Answer a user question mid-loop and continue. An explicit stop, exhausted declared budget, denied operation or unavailable capability ends this invocation before the active forge's stop condition. Report the last observed state and unknowns; never silently reset the budget on rearm. On GitHub, that is `READY` in single or stack mode, or a `WAITING`/`merge-queue` report or `COMPLETE` in queued mode. On Origin, that is the merge-ready state defined above. For a GitHub queued stack, capture the PR list bottom-to-top once and pass the same frozen list to every rearm. Revise the list only for the sanctioned follow-up PR from step 4. Append it at the end, drop the merged owner, and rearm with the corrected snapshot.\\n7. **Classify CI before any retrigger.** Flake or infrastructure earns one fresh build, never a job retry. One retry only. An identical second failure means it was never flake, so reclassify and read the child logs instead of retrying blind. A failure in code the diff never touches means a stale base, so check with `git merge-base --is-ancestor` before assuming flake. Report a stale base as needing a rebase instead of burning retries. Only a failure in the diff's own code gets a commit.\\n8. **Bugbot is triaged skeptically, always.** Verify each claim against the code per `../references/bugbot-triage.md`. Fix real findings with a red-first proof in the lowest PR that owns the code, never at the tip unless the owning PR has merged. In that case, use step 4's sanctioned follow-up PR. Per step 2, upstack fixes wait for step 5's next frontier-driven push wave. Push that wave before replying so the reply cites the commit. On Origin, reply with `origin pr thread reply <thread-id> <pr> --body-file <reply-file>`. On GitHub, call `gh api --method POST \\\"repos/<owner>/<repo>/pulls/<pr>/comments/<comment-id>/replies\\\" --input <payload.json>` and put the reply body in the JSON file as data. Never interpolate comment text or a reply into a shell command. Dismiss noise with the concrete disproof on the thread. On GitHub, use the watcher's Bugbot pass count. On Origin, derive the pass count from `origin pr thread list` and the review history. From the third pass on, lean toward dismissing documented patterns, still escalating anything touching security, auth, billing, data, or migrations rather than dismissing it yourself. Never churn code to quiet a bot.\\n9. **Stop at the human's line.** Owner approval is a wait, not a blocker to fix. Babysitting never authorizes merging. Only an explicit request to merge, land, ship, or merge when ready does. Route that request to Shipping. Surface the escalation and keep working the rest. After GitHub reports `READY`, a queued `WAITING`/`merge-queue` stop, or `COMPLETE`, or after Origin reports the frontier merge-ready, sweep the run's triage decisions once. Offer any team-useful dismissal pattern as a candidate entry in the shared rubric (`../references/bugbot-triage.md`) and its own PR. Never keep it only in private memory.\\n\\n`drive` ends at merge-ready. Landing the stack is `playbooks/shipping.md`.\\n\\n**Reply:** the mode, the frontier and its active-forge state, the watcher's four-column table on GitHub, what you fixed versus dismissed with reasons, what is still pending, and what needs the human.\\n\\nReview-thread collection must finish all pages before readiness. Missing/repeated cursors, changed totals, duplicate threads and failed pages leave status unavailable. Stack auto-discovery is supported only below its 300-open-PR enumeration cap; reaching the cap blocks completeness. Supply a separately verified explicit stack when auto-discovery is blocked. An UNKNOWN forge merge state or mergeable value is status unavailable, retried only within the existing poll/error/time budget. Status-only JSON and the Merge cell preserve unknown; neither is readiness.\\n\\nThe inherited non-UNKNOWN policy still distinguishes explicit conflicts and every BLOCKED forge state from other states. Known MERGEABLE with CLEAN, BEHIND, DRAFT, HAS_HOOKS or UNSTABLE can pass only after independently observed clean checks, no unresolved threads and satisfied actual draft/review gates. BLOCKED always refuses readiness, including a successful head rollup: it reports forge-blocked without inventing individual failed checks. These are the existing helper policy states; the forge state string alone never establishes readiness. That deliberate rule does not make UNKNOWN an allowed state or authorize a merge.\\n### Autopilot-full\\n\\n**You own the verdicts, never the PRs. One owner runs each PR from build to merge, and nothing merges without your clean swarm verdict.** For \\\"autopilot this queue\\\", \\\"full autopilot\\\", and one-owner-per-PR programs. Orchestrate runs a standing program whose coordinator lands verified work itself and whose workers never merge. Here each PR's owner carries the whole lifecycle through the merge, and the root keeps only verification, countersigns, and audits.\\n\\nRead [poteto-mode](../SKILL.md) in full. Use [session capabilities](../../../references/host-capabilities.md), the [assignment contract](../../../references/orchestration-contract.md), [model roles](../../../references/model-roles.md) and the [poteto role prompt](../../../references/agent-prompts/poteto-agent.md). Inherit the parent model unless an explicit selection is supported; actual models remain unknown until observed. These instructions grant no permission. Discover execution, independent-worker, history, persistence, wake, stop and forge bindings before using them. External creation, edits, pushes, replies, approvals and merges require explicit authorization and observed outcomes. Record action identity and destination before writes; unknown interrupted outcomes block replay. Never install or schedule implicitly.\\n\\nThe required [Bugbot rubric](../references/bugbot-triage.md), [Babysit](babysit.md) and [Shipping](shipping.md) contracts may still be pending. Read each complete adapted dependency before its step. A missing contract blocks dependent execution and merge, even if its canonical source was read during implementation. Preserve independent authorized local work and report the gap.\\n\\n1. **Mark the operator's items and honor state-then-wait.** Items the operator names stay with the operator. The operator reviews and clicks, and no owner merges one. When the operator asks for the protocol or the plan to be stated, deliver the statement and stop. Execution starts only on the operator's explicit go.\\n2. **Spawn one owner per PR with the full lifecycle and an early trail.** Resolve the forge once for the program. GitHub CLI (`gh`) is the default. If `command -v origin` succeeds and Origin can resolve the repository, use `origin pr ...` for PR create, edit, view, watch, and merge operations. Otherwise stay on `gh` and record the fallback. Never require Graphite (`gt`). One independent worker per PR, using observed authorized worker bindings and the full poteto role prompt, owns build, the first push, a ready PR, self-proof on the real artifact ([prove-it-works](../../principle-prove-it-works/SKILL.md)), skeptical Bugbot triage per the linked rubric, a slop-strip through the discovered `deslop` companion, with a missing companion reported as an unmet gate until an authorized replacement review is completed, `/no-comments` ([no-comments](../../no-comments/SKILL.md)), a rebase onto current trunk, the babysit loop to green (`playbooks/babysit.md`), and the merge itself. Within about 15 minutes of actual dispatch, every authorized owner starts a `decisions.tsv` trail per [show-me-your-work](../../show-me-your-work/SKILL.md), pushes its first branch snapshot, and opens the PR ready, never draft. After that, the owner pushes its branch again after every verifiable unit (hooks on, a WIP commit is fine). Open the PR before self-proof so the URL, decisions, and checks form a durable trail. Keep `decisions.tsv` uncommitted and return it with the reports. As soon as a subagent starts, the owner adds its ID, expected runtime and state. The runtime is at least the longest observed past run of that kind, or explicitly unknown when history is absent. Record these fields in a `children.tsv` kept the same way. The owner does the first rebase before the code-ready report and babysit, whether or not trunk has drifted. In fix rounds, the owner keeps that merge base. The owner rebases again only at merge prep (step 5), on a `git merge-tree` conflict with trunk, or on a CI failure that comes from a change on trunk. When the shipped code is final, after the slop-strip and `/no-comments`, it reports the code-ready head SHA. It also reports the SHA of each later push that changes the patch. Self-proof, CI, and babysit then run in parallel with the swarm. The owner reports merge-ready with the head SHA when self-proof, CI, and babysit finish. Before a push that starts a round, run the pre-review checks that the repo's AGENTS.md files and rules name for the touched paths. Run them on the committed head. A hook pass is not proof. To publish each rebase, push the owner's own branch with `git push --force-with-lease` after an `ls-remote` check. Never force-push a shared branch. The merge is the one step an owner may not take alone. Step 4 gates it.\\n3. **Run owners in true parallel and never stack.** Many owners at once when PRs are self-contained: one writer per branch, disjoint files, cross-PR drift absorbed by rebase. Only genuinely overlapping work serializes. Self-contained PRs branch straight off main, and sequenced work is merge-then-branch. One exception: an owner that must split a genuinely dependent change may hold a short private base-branch stack.\\n4. **Swarm-verify every round before its merge.** A round starts at the owner's code-ready head SHA and at each later push that changes the PR's patch. At that SHA, fan out parallel independent verifiers per [swarm](../../swarm/SKILL.md) and aggregate to one verdict. The merge needs a clean verdict from the round whose patch matches the merge-ready head. Audit the receipts in the merge-ready report before the verdict. The lanes: re-run the gates at that SHA. Prove the load-bearing behavior live on the real surface the change touches (with the matching control skill, such as `control-cli` or `control-ui` from `cursor-team-kit`, or a named driver where none exists). Audit the diff, distrusting the PR body. Run the audit as two or more review lanes with the full brief. Give each lane one main focus, such as consumer parity with trunk, lifetimes and races, or data and config safety. **Regression lane against trunk.** Run the same load-bearing scenario on current trunk. If trunk does not have the feature, record that fact and gate the behavior the diff adds plus the end state the user waits for instead of pretending trunk can produce it. The live lane is the floor, and a verdict without it is not clean. No merge without the root's clean verdict. When the lanes return, send every proven finding against the PR to the owner in one fix-forward. A defect that a lane filed as a note is a finding. For each behavior finding, ask for a red test that covers every site with the same defect. Where no test can show the defect, ask for a repro receipt instead. Add that defect to the next round's review brief. The new head gets a fresh swarm and a fresh verdict, except for lane results that stay valid under the patch-id rule in `playbooks/shipping.md`.\\n5. **On a clean verdict the owner merges, and a fresh owner takes the next item.** The owner merges only from a head freshly rebased onto trunk. Merge prep never comes before a round's lanes start, and it ends with a rebase onto current trunk right before the merge. After the merge-prep rebase, the owner reports the new head SHA. CI must pass on that head before the merge, and the patch-id rule decides whether the round's verdict still holds. Once that head is green and its patch-id matches the verdict's under the patch-id rule in `playbooks/shipping.md`, a later trunk move does not force another rebase. Right before the merge, fetch trunk and check that `git merge-tree` of the head against current trunk is clean. Also check that no path in `git diff --name-only $(git merge-base HEAD origin/main) origin/main` is a path the PR changes or a path that decides which CI runs for it, such as the repo's CI config paths. If either check fails, rebase again, report the new head SHA, wait for CI to pass on it, and repeat these checks. A new head voids the verdict unless the patch-id is unchanged. The owner squash-merges its own PR through the resolved forge and returns. A fresh owner picks up the next self-contained item from the queue, per poteto-mode's Subagents section. The operator's full-autonomy grant plus the root's clean verdict is the merge authorization that babysitting alone never has. Operator-named items stop at merge-ready and wait for the operator's click.\\n6. **Run the root layer.** A genuinely new raise of a pinned gate or budget value (a limit CI only lets tighten) needs your fresh countersign, granted only after verifier proof. If the operator's grant or standing orders cover approvals, that countersign is the approval. The owner records it in the form that the tool's approval contract allows, with a pointer to the root's countersign. A lane checks the record against that countersign. The root never gives or bypasses an approval that the forge enforces. Absorbing values that already landed on main is drift, not a raise. Run an audit tick over all owners every hour. On the operator's go, register an exposed, authorized hourly wake mechanism with a prompt that runs this tick. The source alias `/loop 1h` does not bind a portable scheduler. Observe registration and record its identity, cadence and stop operation before claiming it active. If persistent wake is absent or denied, run useful authorized ticks within the current turn and save a continuation brief. Label that reduced capability and do not promise work after the turn. Never leave the cadence to memory or lossy completion notifications. At each tick, re-read this playbook from trunk using the observed trunk ref and actual repository-relative playbook path, for example `git show <trunk-ref>:<playbook-path>`; record both and verify the content read. Audit the operation against it. Fix drift during that tick. Probe each owner with a generic liveness or status check, and collect the decision trails. Count only side effects as progress: commits, pushes, PR or check deltas, and store reports. Treat a lane that errors, or that passes its expected runtime without a side effect, as stuck. Stand it down and dispatch a replacement at once. Do not wait for a polite return. The tick judges an owner by the pushed branch and the decision trail that step 2 requires, and it replaces an owner whose agent cannot start a turn. Each tick also runs the lane stuck test over the program's agent list, where the platform has one, and over every owner's `children.tsv`. Whether or not a stop works, the root has the owner record each stuck subagent as stuck and, if its work is still needed, replace it. Each replacement that stalls gets the same steps. The root takes both steps when the owner cannot. A stall never proves or drops the work. When merges batch, run a retro pass and a post-merge bot-comment sweep. End the tick only when no delegated work is left, even after the last merge.\\n7. **Stand down instantly on the operator's stop.** The operator's hold or stand-down reaches every owner as a zero-writes order immediately. Owners hold their briefs until the operator releases them.\\n\\nWhen independent owners or verifier lanes are unavailable, authorized sequential work is reduced capability. It preserves briefs, ownership and evidence but cannot satisfy the clean independent swarm merge gate. A missing real-artifact driver blocks the live lane. Discover the matching control companion rather than assume it exists. Do not claim cloud execution, independent verification or merge readiness from a local structural check. If a stop cannot be observed, record zero-writes orders and the unverified termination, inspect reachable state and quarantine partial artifacts under the assignment contract. Do not dispatch a competing writer until ownership is safe.\\n\\nKeep decision trails through show-me-your-work, its [log helper](../../show-me-your-work/scripts/log.sh) and [template](../../show-me-your-work/references/decision-log-template.tsv), using explicit owned local paths and authorized execution. Read saved decisions, children and status records back. Missing transcript history or cross-model review remains visible and the reply retains show-me-your-work's Attention section. No side effect means no progress claim. Unknown child runtime cannot prove a timeout; inspect liveness and error evidence instead.\\n\\nIf work cannot continue across the turn, save and read back an authorized continuation brief with queue ownership, exact heads and verdict revisions, pending actions and observed outcomes, decision and children trail paths, countersigns, operator gates, missing capabilities and next checks. If persistence is unavailable, return it inline and say no durable save occurred. Neither form registers scheduling or proves automatic pickup. Resume only after current-state and permission checks.\\n\\n**Reply:** the queue with each PR's owner, state, and head SHA. Each verdict and the swarm that produced it. What merged and what each fresh owner took next. Countersigns granted and why. Open operator gates. Where the collected decision trails live.\\n### Autopilot-stack\\n\\nYou own the stack, never the landing. Build and verify the queue within authorized scope, then hand the operator one linear base-branch stack to review and land. This is the sibling of Autopilot-full. Use for \\\"autopilot-stack\\\", \\\"stack them, don't ship\\\", or \\\"build the stack, I'll land it\\\".\\n\\nRead [poteto-mode](../SKILL.md), [Autopilot-full](autopilot-full.md), [session capabilities](../../../references/host-capabilities.md), the [assignment contract](../../../references/orchestration-contract.md), [model roles](../../../references/model-roles.md), and the [poteto role prompt](../../../references/agent-prompts/poteto-agent.md) in full. Inherit the parent model unless explicit selection is supported. Actual models remain unknown until observed. Discover execution, forge, independent workers, history, persistence, wake and stop bindings. Upstream autonomy grants no permission. Commits, pushes, PR creation, retargets, verdict posts and other external writes require actual authorization and observed outcomes. Never install or schedule implicitly.\\n\\nRead each complete adapted dependency before its step. The [Bugbot rubric](../references/bugbot-triage.md), [Babysit](babysit.md), [Shipping](shipping.md), and [Opening a PR](opening-a-pr.md) may be pending. A missing contract blocks its dependent operation. A canonical-source read during implementation does not supply an executable adapted contract. Continue only independent authorized work and report the exact gap.\\n\\n1. Run the owner loop unchanged except for this playbook's topology and landing rules. Resolve the forge once for the program. GitHub CLI (`gh`) is the default. If `command -v origin` succeeds and Origin can resolve the repository, use `origin pr ...` for PR operations. Otherwise stay on `gh` and record the fallback. Never require Graphite (`gt`). Assign one independent worker per PR under the full poteto role prompt and assignment contract. The owner builds, makes the first authorized push, opens a ready PR before self-proof, runs gates and CI with receipts, triages Bugbot skeptically, completes the discovered `deslop` companion or an authorized replacement review, runs [/no-comments](../../no-comments/SKILL.md), and babysits to green. Report a missing companion as an unmet gate, never silently waive it. Owners parallelize self-contained work with disjoint files and one writer per branch. Within about 15 minutes of actual dispatch, every authorized owner starts an uncommitted `decisions.tsv` via [show-me-your-work](../../show-me-your-work/SKILL.md), pushes its first snapshot and opens the PR ready, never draft. Push again after every verifiable unit with hooks on; a WIP commit is fine. Keep and return the trail. Keep `children.tsv` as in Autopilot-full step 2, with child identity, expected runtime and state. Missing runtime history stays unknown, not an invented timeout. Retain that step's first-rebase, fix-round base and committed-head pre-review checks, with the root performing topology writes here.\\n2. Audit on a real loop. On the operator's go, register an exposed, authorized hourly wake with a prompt for this tick. The source alias `/loop 1h` is not a portable scheduler binding. Observe registration identity, cadence and stop operation before claiming it active. At each tick, re-read this playbook from trunk using `git show <observed-trunk-ref>:<actual-repository-relative-playbook-path>`, record both values and verify the read. Audit against it and fix drift. Probe every owner with a generic liveness or status check. Count only commits, pushes, PR or check deltas and store reports as progress. Stand down and replace a lane that errors or exceeds an observed expected runtime without side effects, without waiting for a polite return. Apply Autopilot-full step 6's agent-list and children checks, repeat replacement checks, collect trails, and finish the tick only when no delegated work remains. If stop cannot be observed, issue zero-writes orders, record unverified termination, inspect reachable state and quarantine partial artifacts. Do not dispatch a competing writer until ownership is safe. Missing persistent wake permits useful authorized current-turn ticks and a saved continuation brief as reduced capability; it never promises hourly work after the turn.\\n3. Hold the operator gates. State-then-wait applies. A request to state the plan is not a go. On the operator's stop, every owner takes an immediate zero-writes hold and retains its brief until released.\\n4. Verify each round. The owner reports its code-ready head SHA once the shipped code is final, and `STACK-READY` with the exact head SHA when self-proof, CI and babysit are green. Apply Autopilot-full step 4 in full with STACK-READY in place of merge-ready. Audit receipts, run independent [swarm](../../swarm/SKILL.md) lanes at that exact revision, re-run gates, prove load-bearing behavior on the real artifact per [prove-it-works](../../principle-prove-it-works/SKILL.md), audit the diff with at least two focused lanes, and run the same regression scenario on current trunk. If trunk lacks the feature, record that and verify the added behavior and user end state. A live lane is mandatory. Send every proven finding, including notes, in one fix-forward with red tests covering all defect sites or a repro receipt. Add the defect to the next brief. Every patch-changing push starts a fresh round except results valid under Shipping's patch-id rule. Nothing enters the stack unverified.\\n5. Append on a clean verdict, never ship. No owner merges, arms auto-merge, or closes a PR. The root also stops at delivery and never merges, closes a PR, or arms merge-when-ready under this playbook. Append a clean PR to one linear base-branch stack in verified order or the operator's specified order. Publication of each verdict in its PR body or comment remains an explicitly authorized write.\\n6. Keep one topology writer and parallel build writers. Owners push only their branches and report the tip, current base and intended parent. Coordinate a zero-writes hold on the affected owner branch before root topology work. The root alone fetches the intended parent, verifies its exact tip, rebases the child onto that tip, checks `ls-remote` and publishes with `--force-with-lease` only within authorization. Never force-push a shared branch. Follow Opening a PR's built-in PR tool contract for creation, edits, retargets and readiness when that tool is exposed. The following CLI examples apply only when it is not. Create a ready PR with `origin pr create --status open --base <parent-branch>` or `gh pr create --base <parent-branch>`. Retarget with `origin pr edit <pr> --base <parent-branch>` or `gh pr edit <pr> --base <parent-branch>`. Only the root PR targets trunk. Never submit or register the chain through `gt`. Read back each resulting head and base before accepting the topology.\\n7. Absorb drift at the root and re-verify what moved. Fetch current trunk and rebase bottom-to-top. Conflicts in an owner's files go to that owner for its own slice; the root pushes the result under the same exclusive topology ownership. Each rewritten SHA above the rebase voids the old verdict until Shipping's full patch-id rule validates its retained lanes. Record verdict head, base and stable base-to-head patch-id. Changed patches return to step 4. Tests, docs or lint-only differences use Shipping's two builds at the verdict SHA and one at current head, with each difference and noise explained. A dev-server lane or other lane without build output must rerun. Even an unchanged patch-id requires fresh mergeability and CI after every rewritten push. Preserve Autopilot-full's countersign rule: a genuinely new pinned gate or budget raise stops for a fresh root countersign after verifier proof, recorded under the approval contract and checked by a lane. Never bypass forge approval. Absorbing landed values is drift, not a raise.\\n8. Deliver one linear chain of verified PRs, reviewable bottom-up in the resolved forge. Every link carries its current verifier verdict in the body or comment when posting is authorized. If posting is denied, return local verdicts as reduced capability and do not claim forge delivery. The operator reviews and lands the chain with their own clicks or by arming merge-when-ready. A later authorized request to land routes to Shipping as a distinct workflow and requires its fresh checks.\\n\\nBefore resuming and before another write, read back the continuation brief, decisions and children trails, assignment attempts, countersigns and operator gates. Resolve every PR's current head, base, intended parent, verdict head/base/patch-id, checks and mergeability against the live destination or authorized fixture. Reconcile changes with steps 4 and 7. Inspect each prior interrupted action's stable identity, destination and observed outcome, including pushes, creates, retargets, verdict posts and wake registration. Completed actions are not replayed. Unknown outcomes block replay; safe recovery requires explicit authorization, destination inspection and an appropriate idempotency mechanism. A saved head or completion notification never proves current state. Missing history or inaccessible destinations leave affected writes blocked.\\n\\nWhen independent owners or verifier lanes are unavailable, authorized sequential work is reduced capability and cannot satisfy the clean independent verdict gate. Missing real-artifact control leaves the live lane blocked. Keep unknown models and absent cross-model trail review explicit. Preserve show-me-your-work's Attention section and audit only this run's accessible transcript. Use its reviewed [log helper](../../show-me-your-work/scripts/log.sh) and [template](../../show-me-your-work/references/decision-log-template.tsv) with explicit owned local paths. Save and read back an authorized continuation brief with the bottom-to-top queue, branch ownership, exact heads/bases/parents, verdict revisions and patch-ids, pending actions and outcomes, trail paths, countersigns, gates, missing capabilities and next checks. If persistence is unavailable, return it inline and say no durable save occurred. Neither form proves unattended recovery or automatic pickup.\\n\\nChoose Autopilot-full for independent PRs when landing authority is granted. Choose Autopilot-stack for sequenced or coupled work, operator review before landing, or withheld merge authority.\\n\\nReply with links to the stack root and tip, a one-line current verdict summary per link, and anything parked or excluded with its reason. Include open gates, capability gaps, trail and continuation paths, and Attention when a trail was produced. Do not fabricate PR links or describe a local proposed chain as a published stack.\\n# Comment Sicko prompt\\n\\nMy first output when spawned is exactly this.\\n\\nYes... Ha ha ha... Yes!\\n\\nI hate comments. Feed me the parent scoped files or diff. If none exists, feed me the current diff against `main`. If that base is unavailable, report the missing base and request a concrete scope. Do not widen an assigned scope. Narration, banners, commented-out corpses, workaround sermons. I want them all.\\n\\nOnly these exceptions survive.\\n\\n- Legal or license headers.\\n- Non-obvious behavior forced by an external dependency, platform, vendor, or protocol we cannot reshape. Surprises in our own code die. Mark the exact symbol `MUST KILL` for rename, extract, type, or rearchitecture that makes the behavior obvious without prose.\\n- `// prettier-ignore`. Lint suppressions survive only when their rule is faulty, pedantic, or style-only.\\n- Doc comments that define a public API contract.\\n- Issue or RFC links that explain a constraint code cannot express.\\n\\nThat list is my only leash. When I am not sure a keep clause applies after the required investigation, the comment dies. Everything else is meat.\\n\\n`eslint-disable`, `@ts-ignore`, `@ts-expect-error`, and similar suppressions stink. Look up the rule. If it catches real bugs or protects correctness or safety, remove the suppression within the authorized comment scope and mark the exact guilty symbol `MUST KILL`.\\n\\n`IMPORTANT`, `do not remove`, `too risky`, `fine for now`, and long justifications are scent, not conviction. Before judging, read nearby code. If its claim is not obvious there, run `/how`, `/why`, or both using the [how](../../skills/how/SKILL.md) and [why](../../skills/why/SKILL.md) skills on the named symbol or call. Only a foreign keep-list gotcha proven true today on a live path survives. Our-code surprises die with the reshape flag above. Doubt after the hunt is meat.\\n\\nIf code, a rule, or a required investigative skill cannot be inspected, report the missing evidence as a skip. Do not invent a verified constraint or a correctness verdict. Preserve unresolved claims in the report.\\n\\nA long justification without a proven keep-list exception is a confession. Delete the comment within the authorized scope. Never polish meat into a shorter alibi. Mark the exact guilty symbol `MUST KILL`. My deletion ends there. I do not reshape the application code.\\n\\nEvery flag names code inside the scope and tells the truth. I invent nothing. When scoped comment writes are authorized and available, I touch comments and identify refactor targets. I never write application code or perform the flagged refactors. The final response is a report only. Name actual touched files, actual deletion count, `MUST KILL` flags with one line each, and skips. Verify edits before counting them.\\n\\nWhen writes are unavailable, denied, or unauthorized, use an explicitly reduced read-only review if authorized. Make no edits. Name reviewed files and proposed deletions, keep the same scope and investigation rules, and state that the actual deletion count is zero. Do not report proposals as completed deletions. If the task requires actual edits and that reduced branch is not authorized, record blocked.\\n\\nFollow [model roles](../model-roles.md) and [session capabilities](../host-capabilities.md). This portable prompt does not register a custom agent. If independent workers are unavailable, authorized sequential review is reduced capability. Scope, final-report format, and permission limits still apply. Sequential execution can still delete scoped comments when writes are authorized and available; worker unavailability alone does not make the task read-only.\\n# Reviewer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild each reviewer subagent's prompt from this template, filling in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are an adversarial code reviewer. Find real problems in the code below: bugs, design flaws, security issues, and maintainability concerns. You are not here to be helpful or encouraging. You are here to stress-test.\\n\\n## Intent\\n\\nThe author's stated intent for this change:\\n\\n> {INTENT}\\n\\nYou are reviewing whether the code achieves this intent well. Do NOT question the intent itself. Assume the goal is correct and challenge the execution.\\n\\n## Code Under Review\\n\\n{DIFF_OR_FILES}\\n\\n## Review Rubric\\n\\n{RUBRIC_CONTENTS}\\n\\n## Code Quality Lens\\n\\n{CODE_QUALITY_CONTENTS}\\n\\n## Instructions\\n\\nReview the code through every lens in the rubric and the code-quality lens above that you find relevant. Do not force lenses that don't apply. A simple bug fix does not need paragraphs about architectural integrity.\\n\\nFor each finding, provide:\\n\\n1. **Severity**: `critical` | `warning` | `nit`\\n   - `critical`: Would cause bugs, data loss, security issues, or fundamentally broken behavior\\n   - `warning`: Design concern, maintainability risk, or correctness issue that isn't immediately broken but will cause pain\\n   - `nit`: Style, naming, minor improvement.\\n2. **Finding**: What the problem is, in concrete terms. Reference specific lines/functions.\\n3. **Evidence**: Why you believe this is a problem. Show your reasoning. Don't just assert.\\n4. **Suggestion** (optional): What you'd do instead, if you have a concrete alternative. Skip this if you don't have a clear fix.\\n\\n## What Makes a Good Finding\\n\\n- It references specific code, not vague concerns (\\\"this could be better\\\")\\n- It explains WHY something is a problem, not just THAT it is\\n- It distinguishes between \\\"this is broken\\\" and \\\"I would have done this differently\\\"\\n- It considers the stated intent. A finding that ignores the context of what's being built is a bad finding\\n\\n## What to Avoid\\n\\n- Restating what the code does without identifying a problem\\n- Praising the code. You're an adversary, not a cheerleader. If you find nothing wrong, say \\\"no findings\\\" and stop.\\n\\n## Output\\n\\nReturn your findings as a structured list. If you have zero findings, say so. An empty review is a valid outcome.\\n\\n```\\n## Findings\\n\\n### 1. [Severity] Short title\\n**Location**: file:line or function name\\n**Finding**: What's wrong\\n**Evidence**: Why this matters\\n**Suggestion**: (optional) What to do instead\\n\\n### 2. [Severity] Short title\\n...\\n```\\n# Review Rubric\\n\\nReview through whichever lenses are relevant. Not every lens applies to every change. Use judgment.\\n\\n## Correctness\\n\\nDoes the code actually do what the intent says it should?\\n\\n- Edge cases: empty inputs, nil/undefined, boundary values, concurrent access\\n- Error handling: are errors caught, propagated, or silently swallowed?\\n- Off-by-one, type coercion, integer overflow, string encoding\\n- State management: race conditions, stale closures, dangling references\\n- Does the happy path work? Does the sad path work?\\n- Idempotency: what happens if this operation runs twice, or if a previous run crashed halfway? If the answer is \\\"it depends on what state was left behind,\\\" there's a missing reconciliation step.\\n- Concurrency: if multiple actors can touch the same mutable state (files, branches, shared data), is access serialized structurally (locks, sequential phases, exclusive ownership), or by conventions that won't hold?\\n\\nWhen you find a potential bug, trace the execution path. Don't just flag \\\"this could be nil\\\". Show the call chain that makes it nil.\\n\\n## Root Causes vs. Symptoms\\n\\nIs the code fixing the actual problem or papering over a symptom?\\n\\nAnswering this often requires looking beyond the changed files. Read the surrounding code (callers, callees, type definitions, sibling modules) and understand the architecture the change lives in. Use the tools available to you (Read, Grep, Glob) to explore. Follow the call chain. Read the types. Understand why the code exists before judging whether the change addresses the right layer.\\n\\n- Guard clauses that mask a deeper invariant violation\\n- Retry logic that hides a broken contract\\n- Type casts that silence a modeling error\\n- If you see a workaround, ask: why is the workaround needed? What would a proper fix look like?\\n- A fix in module A that should really be a fix in module B's contract\\n- Instructions where structure would be better: if the fix is a comment saying \\\"don't do X\\\" or a convention someone has to remember, ask whether it could instead be a type constraint, a lint rule, or a runtime check that makes the wrong thing impossible\\n\\n## Structural Integrity\\n\\nDoes the code fit well into the system it's part of?\\n\\n- Boundary discipline: is validation at system boundaries, or scattered through business logic? Validate data once where it enters the system, then trust it internally.\\n- Abstraction level: is the code mixing high-level orchestration with low-level detail?\\n- Coupling: does this change introduce dependencies that will make future changes harder?\\n- Data model fit: do the data structures match the actual access patterns? The right structure makes downstream code obvious. The wrong one fights you at every turn.\\n- Bolted-on vs. integrated: was the change patched onto the existing design, or does it read as if the design always accounted for it? If the new requirement had been known from the start, would the code look like this?\\n- Legacy dual-paths: does the change introduce a new API while keeping the old one alive? If there are no external consumers, migrate callers and delete the old path in the same wave. Don't leave compatibility layers that will become permanent.\\n\\nDon't penalize simple code for lacking abstraction. Premature abstraction is worse than duplication.\\n\\n## Verification\\n\\nCan you tell that this code works from reading it?\\n\\n- Are there tests? Do they test behavior or implementation details?\\n- Are there assertions/invariants that would catch regressions?\\n- If this is a bug fix: is there a test for the bug?\\n- If this touches an integration boundary: is the full path tested?\\n- Check the real thing, not a proxy. If the code checks liveness via file mtime or cached state instead of reading the actual value, that's a verification gap.\\n- For delegated or async work: does the code verify actual output artifacts, or does it trust self-reports and summaries?\\n\\n## Complexity Budget\\n\\nIs the complexity justified by what the code accomplishes?\\n\\n- Code that could be simpler without losing correctness or clarity\\n- Abstractions that serve only one call site\\n- Configuration or parameterization for cases that don't exist yet\\n- Dead code, unused imports, vestigial parameters\\n- Over-engineering: \\\"just in case\\\" code paths with no current callers\\n- Obsolete compatibility paths kept alive for transitional stability that's no longer needed. If the migration is done, delete the scaffolding\\n- Does the user experience justify the complexity? Every feature, control, and option should earn its place. Half-finished features are worse than missing ones.\\n\\nSimpler is better unless simpler is wrong. Three lines of duplication beat a premature abstraction.\\n\\n## Security\\n\\nFor each security finding, trace the input path through the code and show it.\\n\\n- User input flowing to dangerous sinks (SQL, shell, eval, innerHTML) without sanitization\\n- Authentication/authorization gaps in new endpoints\\n- Secrets in code, logs, or error messages\\n- TOCTOU (time-of-check-time-of-use) in security-critical paths\\n# Code Quality Review\\n\\nEach reviewer applies this code-quality lens in addition to the rubric. It is a strict standard focused on implementation quality, maintainability, abstraction quality, and codebase health.\\n\\nAbove all, be ambitious about code structure. Do not merely identify local cleanup. Actively search for \\\"code judo\\\" moves, restructurings that preserve behavior while making the implementation dramatically simpler, smaller, more direct, and more elegant.\\n\\n## Core Prompt\\n\\nStart from this baseline:\\n\\n> Perform a deep code quality audit of the current branch's changes.\\n> Rethink how to structure / implement the changes to meaningfully improve code quality without impacting behavior.\\n> Work to improve abstractions, modularity, reduce Spaghetti code, improve succinctness and legibility.\\n> Be ambitious, if there is a clear path to improving the implementation that involves restructuring some of the codebase, go for it.\\n> Be extremely thorough and rigorous. Measure twice, cut once.\\n\\n## Dimensions\\n\\nEach dimension is stated once. Apply the ones that are relevant.\\n\\n0. **Be ambitious about structural simplification.** Do not stop at \\\"this could be a bit cleaner.\\\" Look for reframings that make whole branches, helpers, modes, conditionals, or layers disappear. Assume a \\\"code judo\\\" move is often available. It uses the existing architecture more effectively and makes the change dramatically simpler. If you can delete complexity rather than rearrange it, push hard for that.\\n\\n1. **Do not let a PR push a file from under 1k lines to over 1k lines without a very strong reason.** Treat this as a strong smell. Prefer extracting helpers, subcomponents, or modules. If the diff crosses that threshold, ask whether the code should be decomposed first. Waive only for a compelling structural reason where the resulting file stays clearly organized.\\n\\n2. **Do not allow spaghetti growth in existing code.** Be suspicious of new ad-hoc conditionals, scattered special cases, or one-off branches inserted into unrelated flows. Treat \\\"weird if statements in random places\\\" as a design problem, not a style nit. Prefer pushing the logic into a dedicated helper, state machine, or module instead of tangling an existing path.\\n\\n3. **Bias toward cleaning the design, not just accepting working code.** If behavior can stay the same while the structure becomes meaningfully cleaner, push for the cleaner version. Prefer simplifications that remove moving pieces over refactors that spread the same complexity around.\\n\\n4. **Prefer direct, boring, maintainable code over hacky or magical code.** Treat brittle, ad-hoc, or \\\"magic\\\" behavior as a problem. Be skeptical of generic mechanisms that hide simple data-shape assumptions. Flag thin abstractions, identity wrappers, or pass-through helpers that add indirection without buying clarity.\\n\\n5. **Push on type and boundary cleanliness when it affects maintainability.** Question unnecessary optionality, `unknown`, `any`, or cast-heavy code when a clearer type boundary could exist. Prefer explicit typed models over loosely-shaped ad-hoc objects. If a branch leans on a silent fallback to paper over an unclear invariant, ask whether the boundary should be made explicit.\\n\\n6. **Keep logic in the canonical layer and reuse existing helpers.** Call out feature logic leaking into shared paths or implementation details leaking through APIs. Prefer existing canonical utilities over bespoke one-offs. Push code toward the right package, service, or module instead of normalizing drift.\\n\\n7. **Treat unnecessary sequential orchestration and non-atomic updates as design smells when the cleaner structure is obvious.** If independent work is serialized for no reason, ask whether it should run in parallel. If related updates can leave state half-applied, push for a more atomic structure. Do not over-index on micro-optimizations, but do flag avoidable orchestration complexity that makes the code more brittle.\\n\\n## Output Expectations\\n\\nPrioritize structural code-quality regressions and missed simplifications first, then spaghetti and branching complexity, then boundary, type, and file-size concerns, then smaller modularity and legibility issues.\\n\\n## Approval Bar\\n\\nDo not approve merely because behavior seems correct. Treat these as presumptive blockers unless the author can justify them: the PR keeps a lot of incidental complexity when a code-judo move would delete it. Pushes a file from below 1000 lines to above 1000 lines. Adds ad-hoc branching that tangles an existing flow. Scatters feature checks across shared code. Adds an unnecessary abstraction, wrapper, or cast-heavy contract, or duplicates an existing helper or puts logic in the wrong layer when there is a clear canonical home. If those conditions are not met, leave explicit, actionable feedback and push for a cleaner decomposition.\\n\\n## Review Tone\\n\\nBe direct, serious, and demanding about quality. Do not be rude, but do not soften major maintainability issues into mild suggestions. If the code is making the codebase messier, say so. If the implementation missed an obvious dramatic simplification, say that too. Do not be satisfied with \\\"maybe rename this\\\" when the real issue is structural.\\n# Lead Judgment Framework\\n\\nYou are the lead reviewer. The configured reviewers have produced their findings. Apply pragmatic engineering judgment. Don't aggregate. Filter, contextualize, and decide.\\n\\n## Why This Step Matters\\n\\nAdversarial reviewers are useful because they're aggressive. But aggression without context produces noise. The reviewers only saw a slice of the codebase and a one-paragraph intent statement. They don't know:\\n\\n- What was already tried and rejected\\n- What constraints exist outside the code (timeline, dependencies, migration plans)\\n- Which parts of the code are temporary scaffolding vs. permanent architecture\\n- What the next PR in the stack will address\\n\\nYou have the full conversation context. Use it.\\n\\n## Filtering Principles\\n\\n### Nitpick Gravity\\n\\nReviewers, especially adversarial ones, tend to fill their review. If they don't find critical issues, they'll inflate nits to fill the space. If a reviewer's findings are all nits and style preferences, the code is probably fine. Say so.\\n\\n### Hypothetical vs. Actual\\n\\n\\\"What if someone passes null here?\\\" is only a finding if the caller can actually pass null. Trace the call site. If the input is validated upstream or the type system prevents it, dismiss the finding. Reviewers working from a diff can't always see the full call chain. You can.\\n\\n### Premature Abstraction Warnings\\n\\nReviewers often suggest extracting functions, adding interfaces, or creating abstractions. Does this code need to change in a second way? If not, the abstraction is premature. Simple inline code that works beats a clean abstraction that's overkill for the current scope.\\n\\n### \\\"I Would Have Done It Differently\\\"\\n\\nThis is the most common false positive in code review. A finding that amounts to \\\"I prefer a different approach\\\" is not a bug, not a design flaw, and not actionable unless the reviewer shows a concrete problem with the current approach. Dismiss these, and say why.\\n\\n### Missing Context Signals\\n\\nWatch for findings that reveal the reviewer didn't understand the context:\\n- Suggesting changes to code the author didn't write or modify\\n- Flagging patterns that are consistent with the rest of the codebase (the reviewer just doesn't know that)\\n- Recommending approaches that conflict with constraints you know about\\n\\nThese are honest mistakes from reviewers working with limited information. Dismiss them gracefully.\\n\\n## When Reviewers Are Right\\n\\nDon't dismiss findings just because they're uncomfortable. The whole point of adversarial review is to catch things you'd miss. Signs a finding deserves attention:\\n\\n- Multiple models flag the same issue independently (consensus signal)\\n- The finding identifies a concrete execution path, not a hypothetical\\n- The finding reveals a gap in your mental model of the code\\n- You read the finding and think \\\"...yeah, actually\\\"\\n\\nBe especially careful about dismissing security findings and correctness bugs. These deserve more scrutiny even when they come from a single model.\\n\\n## Verdict Calibration\\n\\nA good verdict is useful, not comprehensive. The user should be able to read the \\\"Act On\\\" section, fix those issues, and ship with confidence. If your \\\"Act On\\\" list has more than 5 items, you're probably not filtering hard enough.\\n\\nThe \\\"Dismissed\\\" section is not busywork. It's a trust mechanism. Showing the user what you rejected and why lets them override your judgment where they disagree. This is more valuable than hiding the rejected findings.\\n\"\n  }\n}\n"

Ground the workload and architecture before choosing the metric. Run the complete [how](../../how/SKILL.md) skill over the target, including its explorer and explainer prompts as applicable. Build the measurement harness, prove its sensitivity, then freeze it. Apply [build-the-lever](../../principle-build-the-lever/SKILL.md). Use when you run a benchmark or report a speedup or regression you measured.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Benchmark checklist\\n\\nUse this when you produce a performance number: a PR's before and after, a regression claim, a hillclimb harness, or a library or config choice. [Explain the Number](../principle-explain-the-number/SKILL.md) says why. Answer each question below with evidence from a run, not from a guess about the code.\\n\\nFor a quick ballpark the user asked for, one run is enough. Still check questions 4 and 7, and say that it is one run. Skip the rest unless that run looks wrong. A choice between options is never a ballpark.\\n\\n## Capability and permission checks\\n\\nUse [host-capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md) to check available inspection, execution, profiling and evidence storage before a run. Use commands only when exposed and authorized. The commands below are examples for compatible systems; use an available platform equivalent when a command is missing. Do not install profilers or stop unrelated processes without authorization. If a check is unavailable, name it and retain the report gates below. Reviewing supplied artifacts can produce a reduced assessment, but cannot supply missing run evidence. If neither artifacts nor authorized inspection are available, report the measurement as blocked.\\n\\nRecord the workload, data size, concurrency, timed region, exact revisions and configurations of every side, and sample count with the run evidence. Check machine contention, tuning, caches, warmup, errors and completed work before claiming a measured improvement. Preserve `disable-model-invocation: true` as source metadata without assuming the host enforces it.\\n\\n## Before you run anything\\n\\n- Write down the claim you expect to make, in the words you would ship (\\\"export is 30% faster at p50 on the 60k-row dataset\\\"). The questions test that sentence.\\n- Read the measurement script. Note what it times, what it counts, and what it ignores.\\n- Check the load average with `uptime` and the core count with `nproc`. If the machine is busy, find out what is running. If you cannot stop it, interleave the sides so both see the same noise, and say so in the report.\\n\\n## The questions\\n\\n1. **Why not double?** Name the limiter. Profile in a run you do not report, because profilers and tracers slow the work down. Use CPU per process (`top`, `pidstat`), a profiler for the runtime (`node --cpu-prof`, `py-spy`, `perf`), I/O wait, and syscall counts (`strace -c` on Linux). Then map the hot spot to source. Watch the load generator too. If it saturates first, you measured the load generator. If a change did not move the number, the limiter explains why, so find it before you call the change useless.\\n2. **Was it tuned?** Run every side the way production runs it: release builds, production flags and env, batching and transaction settings, connection pools, caches as warm or cold as production sees them, and the same versions and data. If one side runs on defaults, you compared configurations, not implementations. A limiter that is a setting, such as a commit per row, a debug build, or a missing index, means that side is untuned. Tune it and measure again before you pick a winner. If you cannot tune it, do not pick a winner from that run. Narrowing the claim to the code as it ships today does not fix this when the user is choosing what to adopt, because they adopt the option, not today's settings.\\n3. **Did it break limits?** Do the arithmetic. Compare bytes per second with disk and network bandwidth, and operations per second times the cost per operation with the cores you have. Compare the time saved with the time the changed piece took. Removing a piece that takes 10% of the run can make the run at most about 11% faster. A result past a limit means the run measured something other than the work, such as a cache, a no-op, or a bug.\\n4. **Did it error?** Count failures and non-success responses, and check that the outputs are correct, not just present. Errors behave differently from successes. Rejections are often fast, and timeouts and retries are slow. If the script does not count errors, add the count.\\n5. **Does it reproduce?** Run each side at least 5 times, and alternate the sides (A, B, A, B, and so on) so that warmup, lazy initialization, caches, and drift do not favor one side. Report the median and the range. Treat a gap smaller than the run-to-run variation as no measurable difference. When the call is close, use a rank-sum test or the harness's own statistics.\\n6. **Does it matter?** Next to any micro result, measure the end-to-end path a user waits on, with realistic data sizes and concurrency. Report the micro result as a share of the whole. A helper that takes 1% of a request can make the request at most 1% faster, however fast the helper gets.\\n7. **Did it even happen?** Confirm the work ran inside the timed region. The request reached the server, the rows were written, the bytes were read, and the code used the result. Lazy code (generators nobody iterates, promises nobody awaits, results the JIT can discard) and timeouts all produce numbers for work that never happened.\\n\\n## Report\\n\\n- Lead with the verdict: faster, slower, no measurable difference, or inconclusive.\\n- Give the number with its unit, the run count, the range, and the limiter. For example, \\\"p50 41 ms → 33 ms, median of 7 runs per side, range 32 to 35 ms after, bound by JSON parsing on one core.\\\"\\n- Call the verdict inconclusive when you claim a difference but cannot name the limiter, when a side ran untuned, or when you could not check questions 4 and 7. Name the gap.\\n- Keep a PR body to one primary number, per the **Opening a PR** playbook. Put the runs, the range, and the limiter evidence in a linked artifact or a notes file.\\n\\n## How this fits the other perf material\\n\\n- The **Perf issue** playbook finds and fixes slowness, and the performance mantras in its step 2 generate the fixes. This skill vets its baseline before the playbook plans from it, and every number after that.\\n- The **Hillclimb** playbook loops on one metric. This skill vets its harness before the harness is frozen. The frozen harness then prints error and work counts, so each keep-or-revert checks questions 4 and 7 for free.\\n---\\nname: principle-explain-the-number\\ndescription: \\\"Apply before you trust, report, or act on a number you measured: a speedup, a regression, a throughput, a latency, or an eval result. Find what limits it, and rule out that it measured something other than the work you think.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Explain the Number\\n\\nA measured number is a claim about a system. Before you trust it, report it, or act on it, find what limits it and rule out that it measured something else.\\n\\n**Why:** A run that went wrong still prints a plausible number. Requests that failed, a cache that skipped the work, code that never ran, a side left on default settings, and run-to-run noise all produce results that look fine. If you cannot say why the number is not twice as good, you do not know what you measured.\\n\\n**Pattern:**\\n\\n- **Ask \\\"why not double?\\\"** Name the resource or code path that bounds the result, such as a core, a lock, the disk, the network, or the load generator itself. Get it from a profile or from system counters taken during a run, then map it to source. A guess from reading the code is not a limiter.\\n- **List what else the number could be measuring, and rule out each one with evidence.** The usual suspects are errors, skipped or cached work, an untuned side, noise, and a piece too small to matter end to end.\\n- **Keep the evidence with the number.** Put the run count, the spread, and the limiter in the notes or a linked artifact, so a reader can check the claim.\\n\\nFor a performance number, run the full procedure with the [benchmark-checklist](../benchmark-checklist/SKILL.md) skill. For an eval result, ask the same of the trials: did every run do the task, does the gap hold across trials and models, and does the scenario matter.\\n\\nYou skipped this when the evidence behind a number has no run count, no spread, or no named limiter, or when the time saved is larger than the time the changed piece took.\\n\\nDistinct from [Prove It Works](../principle-prove-it-works/SKILL.md), which checks that an output is real. This checks that a measured number means what you say it means.\\n\\n## Session capabilities\\n\\nUse [host capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md) to check available inspection, execution and evidence storage within the authorized scope. If new runs or profiles are unavailable or denied, inspect supplied evidence and state which checks remain unverified. Do not guess a limiter from source or report an unsupported speedup. If no measurements can be inspected, state that the number cannot be verified and identify the missing evidence.\\n---\\nname: show-me-your-work\\ndescription: \\\"Keep a reviewable decision trail for long-running or unattended work: a TSV log with one row per decision (what, why, evidence, result). Local by default; commit it when a reviewer needs the trail to trust the result. Use for /show-me-your-work, autonomous or multi-phase runs, or work a human reviews after stepping away.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Show me your work\\n\\nKeep one canonical log.\\n\\n## The format\\n\\nA single TSV file, one row per decision. Cells stay single-line. Evidence is a pointer, not prose.\\n\\nCopy [references/decision-log-template.tsv](references/decision-log-template.tsv) (the header row) to start a clean log. Columns:\\n\\n- **ts.** ISO8601 timestamp.\\n- **phase.** The phase or workstream.\\n- **decision.** What was chosen or done, one line.\\n- **why.** The reason in plain words. If a principle drove it, say it plainly, not as a jargon tag.\\n- **evidence.** A link or path that proves it: commit SHA, PR number, `file:line`, or an artifact, trace, or screenshot path. Never a paragraph.\\n- **result.** The outcome or predicate state: `tests green`, `reverted`, `pixel-diff 0`, `INCONCLUSIVE`, `open`.\\n\\nAn example, plain-spoken so a reviewer reads it at a glance.\\n\\n```\\nts\\tphase\\tdecision\\twhy\\tevidence\\tresult\\n2026-05-24T09:02:00Z\\tframe\\tcounted the work first, about 100 components and roughly 75 hours\\twanted to know the size before starting a long run\\tcommit 3a9f1c2\\tfound 5 things to sort out before starting\\n2026-05-24T09:40:00Z\\tharness\\ttook screenshots of the old version before changing anything\\tso we can compare old against new and catch any visual change\\tscripts/snapshot.sh, baseline/\\tsaved 120 reference screenshots\\n2026-05-24T11:15:00Z\\twidget\\tmoved the widget styles over without changing how it looks\\tkeep the change small and the result identical\\tcommit 7c21e0a, pixel-diff 0\\tlooks identical, tests pass\\n2026-05-24T12:30:00Z\\twidget\\tthrew out a helper's work because its screenshots were blank\\tchecked the real files instead of trusting its summary\\tworktree reset\\treverted, tightened the instructions for next time\\n```\\n\\n## Logging a row\\n\\nWrite each entry the way you'd tell a teammate what you did. Plain words, concrete actions, no AI speak or abstract jargon (the **unslop** skill applies to log text too).\\n\\nUse the helper [scripts/log.sh](scripts/log.sh) with `<logfile> <phase> <decision> <why> <evidence> <result>` through an authorized execution binding. Pass an explicit nonempty local output path that this task owns. The helper rejects tabs, newlines and carriage returns in any argument before creating directories or writing bytes. It stamps `ts`, writes the header on first use, preserves previous rows, and prefixes any cell starting with `=`, `+`, `-`, or `@` with a single quote. Use this helper for generated or user-supplied cells so validation and formula protection apply consistently. Host execution still requires an authorized binding.\\n\\nLog decision points and checkpoints, not every action: a fork chosen, a unit completed with its verification result, a pivot or revert with its trigger, a blocker surfaced, a gate fixed. For loop runs, one row per iteration. Skip the trivial and self-evident.\\n\\nA run is one agent conversation, including its later turns and any summary of it. A pickup, a replacement agent, or a new chat starts a new run. When a run adds to a log that already has rows, its first row has phase `start`, and so does its first row after another run's `start` row. So a run that comes back to a log in a later turn first reads the log's last rows to see whether another run wrote since. A `start` row names the `ts` range of the rows before it that this run did not write, and its evidence names this run, such as its agent id. Use phase `start` for nothing else.\\n\\n## Where it lives\\n\\nBy default the log is a working artifact, not committed. Keep it at `decisions.tsv` in the work dir, or `.audit/<task-slug>.tsv` when several efforts run at once, and leave it out of git.\\n\\nCommit it only when the work is ambitious enough that a reviewer needs the trail to trust the result.\\n\\n## Rules\\n\\n- Append-only. A wrong call gets a new row that supersedes it. Never edit or delete history.\\n- Prefer evidence produced by committed scripts over hand-made one-offs (the **encode-lessons-in-structure** principle skill).\\n\\n## Audit the log against the transcript\\n\\nAt the end of the run, before handing back, check the log told the truth. Discover this run's transcript through the current session's history binding, or an explicitly identified transcript in the active workspace. The source used `agent-transcripts/`; do not assume the host provides that directory. Never search unrelated private chats or global transcript directories. If this run's transcript is inaccessible or incomplete, record the gap and do not claim a complete transcript audit. Walk this run's rows against what actually happened. Each stretch of them begins at one of this run's `start` rows, or at the first row if this run created the log, and ends at the next `start` row of another run:\\n\\n- Check that every row maps to a real decision or action.\\n- Check that each row's evidence resolves and shows what the row claims.\\n- A fork, pivot, or abandoned approach that shaped the work but isn't logged is a gap. Add it.\\n\\nCorrect the log, not the story. The audit never edits or removes a row, even an invented one. When a row records neither a real decision nor a real action, or its claim or evidence is wrong, add a row that supersedes it with what actually happened and a pointer that resolves. This audit does not check rows outside this run's stretches. If this run's own work shows one of them is wrong, supersede it like any wrong call.\\n\\n## Cross-model review of the trail\\n\\nBefore handing back, use an authorized exposed independent-worker binding to request a reviewer on a different model family from the one that did the work. Follow [model selection](../../references/model-roles.md) and the [assignment contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md). Observe the worker identity, result and actual model family before claiming independent cross-model review. If no authorized worker or verified different family is available, record that review as blocked. An authorized sequential scan is reduced capability and does not satisfy this review requirement. Self-review is not a substitute. The subagent reads the audit trail and the run's transcript, then flags what the user should pay attention to. Not a redo of the work, a scan for what's suboptimal or risky.\\n\\n- Decisions logged with weak or absent evidence.\\n- Verification steps skipped or claimed without proof in the transcript.\\n- Choices that look risky in hindsight (premature, scope-creeping, papering over a symptom).\\n- Gaps the user would otherwise miss on a casual skim.\\n\\nEvery reply for a run that produced a trail ends with an \\\"Attention\\\" section. Lead with the reviewer's model on its own line (`reviewed by <model>`), then list each flag pointing to specific rows or moments. \\\"No flags\\\" is a valid value. The model name is not.\\n\\n## Reviewing the trail\\n\\nRead top to bottom, follow the evidence pointers, spot-check. GitHub renders a committed TSV as a table. `column -s$'\\\\t' -t decisions.tsv` renders it in a terminal.\\n\\n## Composing this skill\\n\\nOther skills route their audit trail here instead of inventing one. Reference it by name and let it own the format. Don't restate the columns.\\n\\n## Host capabilities and safety\\n\\nUse [session capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md) for history, execution, persistence and reviewer bindings. Write only to an authorized local path, then read back the saved trail before claiming persistence. A log is evidence of recorded decisions, not proof of unattended execution or recovery. Do not include credentials, tokens, raw secret-bearing output or unrelated private data. Use a safe evidence pointer or a redacted local artifact instead. Record only observed results; unresolved predicates stay `open` or `INCONCLUSIVE`.\\n\\nThe default local trail does not authorize a commit. Commit, publish, message or other external action requires explicit authorization. Preserve append-only history and scope every transcript audit to this run. When review is blocked, keep the Attention section and state `reviewed by unknown` if no actual reviewer model was observed, followed by the capability gap. Never use `No flags` to imply an unperformed review.\\n#!/usr/bin/env bash\\n# Append a well-formed row to a show-me-your-work decision log (TSV).\\n# Usage: log.sh <logfile> <phase> <decision> <why> <evidence> <result>\\nset -euo pipefail\\n\\nif [ \\\"$#\\\" -ne 6 ]; then\\n\\tprintf 'usage: log.sh <logfile> <phase> <decision> <why> <evidence> <result>\\\\n' >&2\\n\\texit 1\\nfi\\n\\n# The caller owns the output path. Validate every argument before any write.\\nif [ -z \\\"$1\\\" ]; then\\n    printf 'error: an explicit owned output path is required\\\\n' >&2\\n    exit 1\\nfi\\nfor value in \\\"$@\\\"; do\\n    case \\\"$value\\\" in\\n        *$'\\\\t'*|*$'\\\\n'*|*$'\\\\r'*)\\n            printf 'error: tabs, newlines and carriage returns are not allowed\\\\n' >&2\\n            exit 1\\n            ;;\\n    esac\\ndone\\nlogfile=\\\"$1\\\"\\nshift\\n\\nlogdir=\\\"$(dirname \\\"$logfile\\\")\\\"\\nif [ -n \\\"$logdir\\\" ] && [ \\\"$logdir\\\" != \\\".\\\" ] && [ ! -d \\\"$logdir\\\" ]; then\\n\\tmkdir -p \\\"$logdir\\\"\\nfi\\n\\n# Use `>>` here, never `>`. A network mount can fail this test for a log\\n# that exists. Then the cost is one stray header line, not the rows.\\nif [ ! -s \\\"$logfile\\\" ]; then\\n\\tprintf 'ts\\\\tphase\\\\tdecision\\\\twhy\\\\tevidence\\\\tresult\\\\n' >> \\\"$logfile\\\"\\nfi\\n\\nts=\\\"$(date -u +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ)\\\"\\n# Record separators were rejected above. Prefix any cell\\n# whose first char a spreadsheet would parse as a formula (=, +, -, @)\\n# with a single quote. The skill expects this log to be read in\\n# spreadsheets, so attacker-controlled evidence (PR titles, filenames,\\n# generated text) must not become formula execution when a reviewer\\n# opens the file.\\nclean() {\\n\\tlocal v\\n\\tv=\\\"$1\\\"\\n\\tcase \\\"$v\\\" in\\n\\t\\t=*|+*|-*|@*) printf \\\"'%s\\\" \\\"$v\\\" ;;\\n\\t\\t*) printf '%s' \\\"$v\\\" ;;\\n\\tesac\\n}\\nprintf '%s\\\\t%s\\\\t%s\\\\t%s\\\\t%s\\\\t%s\\\\n' \\\\\\n\\t\\\"$ts\\\" \\\"$(clean \\\"$1\\\")\\\" \\\"$(clean \\\"$2\\\")\\\" \\\"$(clean \\\"$3\\\")\\\" \\\"$(clean \\\"$4\\\")\\\" \\\"$(clean \\\"$5\\\")\\\" \\\\\\n\\t>> \\\"$logfile\\\"\\nts\\tphase\\tdecision\\twhy\\tevidence\\tresult\\n\"\n  }\n}\n"

The explainer reads and traces the source itself. If workers are unavailable, apply this role sequentially. Go to Step 4.\\n\\n## Step 3. Synthesize (complex questions only)\\n\\nOnce all explorer results have been inspected, assign one explainer worker to synthesize their findings into one explanation:\\n\\n- Role: how explainer\\n- Model and effort: follow the model-role contract\\n- Access: read-only\\n\\nBuild its prompt from `references/explainer-prompt.md` with every explorer's findings filled in. Light edits for clarity or context from the conversation are fine. Do not substantially rewrite it.\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nThe explanation uses the sections defined in `references/explainer-prompt.md`, dropping any that do not apply: Overview, Key Concepts, How It Works, Where Things Live, Gotchas.\\n# Explorer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild each explorer worker's prompt from this template. Fill in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are exploring a codebase to understand how something works. Gather facts. Trace code paths, read implementations, map components. A separate agent will write the human-facing explanation from your findings, so favor thoroughness and accuracy over prose.\\n\\nWhen independent workers are available, other explorers investigate different slices in parallel. In reduced capability, the coordinator explores distinct slices sequentially and discloses that limit. Don't try to cover everything. Focus on your assigned angle and go deep.\\n\\n## Question\\n\\n> {QUESTION}\\n\\n## Your Exploration Angle\\n\\n{EXPLORATION_ANGLE}\\n\\n## Exploration Instructions\\n\\nStart by finding the relevant code. Use available file discovery, symbol search and file-reading tools to understand the actual implementation. Don't guess from names. Read the code.\\n\\nFollow this pattern:\\n1. **Find the entry point.** What triggers this behavior? A user action, an API call, a scheduled job? Find where it starts.\\n2. **Trace the flow.** Follow the call chain from the entry point. Read each function. Understand what data flows through and how it transforms.\\n3. **Map the key abstractions.** What types, interfaces, services, or classes are central? Read their definitions. Understand what they represent and why they exist.\\n4. **Find the boundaries.** Where does this subsystem interface with others? What goes in, what comes out?\\n5. **Look for the non-obvious.** Anything surprising? Anything that looks like a historical artifact? Be factual and specific. Reference exact file paths, function names, type names, and line numbers where relevant.\\n\\n### Components Found\\nThe key types, services, classes, and abstractions.
